ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

Somehow or other any great social event, whethes in our own particular society set or in one much above it, tends to give a stimulus to affairs sartorial, and the Coronation is influencing the modistic world at present. Even the poorest woman in the United Kingdom will on the 26th of June do her best to make as brave a show as possible, and by some little touch. of finery, such as a new ribbon or feather, evince her loyalty and innate sense;of the requirements, of the occasion. The great event is as a stone dropped into the pool of Society, and the ripples, lessening in strength as they go, will gently agitate all the world of dress. The peeress will don her diamonds and robes to do honour to her sovereigns, while the " donah " will also appear in gorgeous if not such tasteful or expensive garb; still it will have cost her much more thought and care than did that worn by her wealthier sister. There is little else talked of in any grade of society save this great national event, until, indeed, one is almost weary of the word " Coronation " as applied even to articles of dress and all the little etceteras of the toilette.. Red is a colour which has naturally enough sprung into fashionable favour, and is a note of colour which, if tastefully introduced, is exceedingly effective and pleasing. A red hat with a parasol to match is always very chic, while even the red parasol, taken singly, will brighten up many a costume, and add to its attractions. A grass lawn gown such as illustrated is much enhanced by a red sunshade and hat, while a further note of red is continued in the soft accordion-pleated tie and ends which finish the wide

MASS LAWN GOWN.

collar of white embroidery. Triple frills of the same embroidery decorate theisem of the skirt, and form the cuffs at the wrists and the bands on the upper part of the arms. Lace collars are much worn, a fact to rejoice the heart of the economi- cally-minded woman, for, by possessing herself of one or two of these dainty trifles of coarse or fine lace or embroidery, one can ring the changes on an every-day costume or blouse and make it appear very smart. The newest and perhaps the prettiest collars just now are of coarse linen or grass lawn with alternate squares of coarse Irish guipure flowers standing up in bold relief. There 'is infinite scope in th2se dainty trifles for the home worker to display both originality and taste, for, by obtaining a piece of lawn or linen, and perhaps a yard or so of a boldly-patterned lace insertion, a girl can evolve a collar which will give quite an air of style to a toilette. Some collars are made of dull, neutral - tinted ribbed silk with delicate flowers and buds in silk embroidery scattered apparently carelessly over the surface. The smart little hat depicted is of the ornate variety of 'sailor hat so much in vogue tlaig season. It is trimmed with a band of navy bliss

A USEFUL HAT.

foulard spotted with white, which also forms a smart bow at the back and the ends, which fall over the coiffure at the back, and are finished by a couple of gold balls. A dull gold buckle is arranged at one side. Hats this season, espe- cially those of the more dressy variety, are flat as to crown and placed rather far back on the head, with a wide brim projecting over the face in front. The brims are literally covered with wreaths and bunches of very natural - looking flowers and foliage, while a swathing of lace and silk intermingled surrounds the crown. The brim is most becoming when curved and bent to suit the face of the wearer. It is easy to make such a hat at home as one can for a couple of shillings (or more if a better straw is desired) purchase a large floppy straw shape which is neatly interthreaded with wire to shape it and keep it firm. The floral decora- tions can be purchased ready to put on, and the :ace and silk adjusted to taste by the aid of a few ornamental pins.

This useful and at the same time comfortable coat is made of putty coloured taffetas with a large collar of white silk strapped with satin

A COMFORTABLE COAT.

of the same hue as the taffetas. The sleeves are lined with white silk and are strapped with the satin, while large ornamental buttons form the fastening. For travelling by train or by road a coat of this description will be, found invalu- able, as it protects the dainty toilette under- neath from dust or from becoming soiled readily. Some people prefer the coat without the touch of white, but, unless the wearer is possessed of a brilliant complexion, the dull tone of the taffetas will be found somewhat trying. The taffetas coat is the latest outdoor wrap for summer wear, and some of the models are made with accordion pleats falliug from the neck to below the knees, and in some inbtances a girdle of black velvet is loosely tied round the waist. The dust coat of the present day is a very different garment from the unsightly object of a few jeers ago which was possessed of neither style nor fit, and only served to conceal any preten• slops to a good figure the wearer might lay claim to. Some of the most fashionable ladies have these wraps made of white taffetas, but that is too easily soiled to stand much wear.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

It le an error to assume that we are all the dutifull subjects of the Queen, though the mistake is being commonly made. The other day an address was pre- sented to bee Majetty by her "loyal subjects" and in one of the current sixpenny illustrated papers there is a picture of the Queen standing at one of the window) of Buckingham Palace. The occasion was Peace Sunday, when a great crowd assembled outside the Mace, and the picture is entitled " The Queen and her Subjects." This acknowledgement however is due tot eKing only, the Queen being our fellow subject, just tbe Fame as she was in tbe reign of Queen Victoria. We had become so accustomed to a reigning Queen, that some people find it difficult to realise that they are the rut jocts of ! King Edward and of his Majesty OEly. The Thanksgiving Service in St Paul's Cathedral as moit impressive and as may be imagined, an immense congregation attended to take part in it. Their Majesties, the King end Queen, were accompanied by a large gathering of the Royal family, including tha Prince and Princess of Wales, the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, Princess Christian, Princes. Victoria, and Prince and Princess Charles of Denmark. The weather was anytbine but favourable but an enormous throng ' assembled along the route to witness the passing of the , King and Queen, and Royal personages. Many peers ' I and peeresses were also present at the service at which the rerdering of the Ti Daum to the special setting of the late Sir Arthur Sullivan was exceedingly grand. In addition to the new Earl of Chichester there are several peers who are in Holy Orders, includiag the Earl of Devon, the Earl of Bessborougb, the Earl of Strafford, Lord Scarsdale. and Lord Normaady, who is a Canon of Windsor. The Rev Francis 0 Pelham who now becomes Earl of Chichester in censequeece of the death of his elder brother. is Vicar if Yarmouth. 'There are alse several peers who though net in Holy Orders, are well known pulpit orators. Viscount Halifax, the leader of the advanced Ritt,absts. and the Earl of Tankers ille are both exceedingly atle preachers and amongst others whomiabt he mentioned in this category are Lord Kin- naird, Lord Overtcue, and Lord Radsteca. The British climate as this year mostly been in its worst mood, and some fears may be sntrrtair sod that it will unfavourably imprees the Coronation guests. Many people are disposed to think that the King has not inherited the good fortune of our late Queen, who was invariably favoured with fine weather, and certaisly most of his Majesty's appearances in public this year, have been under wet or clouded skies. At the Epsom , races, all of which the Eirg attended, there *maid only have been one worse day than that of the Derby, and that was the Oaks, at which women are expected to assume their bravest attire. So bad Wfta the weather that neither the Queen or Prineeas of Wales attended. and many who did so returned with their frocks and furbelows all more or less in a state of wreckage. Fashionable society never patronises Epsom as it deer Ascot or Sandown, but there, on such occaeioas, every- thing depends upon fine weather, and without it tbe best of toilettes becomes a failure Some impetus to the approach of the short skirt seems to come from Paris, where it is being adopted by tee very smartest women for morning wear, and even for Park tcilettes. There can be no doubt, as between tbe long and the short skirt as to which is the more con- venient, but this is a small consideration with women when it is a question of elegar es and for this reason the comisg of the shorter skirt has been slow, and by ne means sure. Bet its beirg taken up by women. who never wear anything but the fathions for the day af!sr to-morrow, will quickly alter matters, ard this is the prospect that now seems held out to us from Paris. White gloves are in higher favor than ever before. It wculd be well however if tbe mode was patronised by those women only who can afford it, and furthemare blesee with small, well-shaped bands. Dark gloves are infinitely better than soiled or much cleaned white oner, gni if the bands they cover are large they do not draw attention to the fact. Happily the idea of squeezing the hand into gleves a sir', or two smaller than it really nerds, is ore that may be numbered 'among there past and gone. To-day such a proceeding is deemed vulgar. In the reenning of gloves, cotton and not silk should be used, as the latter invariably cuts the kid or suede. ' All sewing moreover should be done on the right side. When holes are concerned these should never be drawn together, a pleas of kid, or failing this, silk, of the same colour, should be tacked underneath, tie glove being next turned on the right side, and the tern edges neatly buttonholed and pressed. In removing gloves, they should be unbuttoned, and drawn ever the hands, the Angers, which "ill then be on the wrong side, bsine tumid, and carefully pulled out as soon as possible. Ti remove one's gloves by pulling at the finger tips, 'pills ruination to the very best cut specimens. iSaStiff rustling silks that " stand alone " are distinetly out of date at the moment, even erreng the mcst sow- servative of dowagers. Instead, we are all donning soft, rich-looking crêpe de chines, soft double-with satins, and the new uncrusbable, soft-finish glace silks. Then there are the cool-looking tussah and Shrirituez silks, and the many varieties of thin washing silks, of home, Chinese, and Japanese weaving. The latter is its great demand this summer. There is one question which is of more iniumriance to housewivea than either the Coronation or the late war, and that is the ecareity of domeatic servant,. It was recently reported form Lincolnekire that there were marcely any women servants to be ootsined at the kir• jog fairs, awl from all parts of the country the store is the same. In these circumstances acme people are asking why it should be necessary to rairefueos for the purpose of sending girls to Canada,but that is only a small factor in the case, and there seems to be every reason to apprehend that the difficulty of obtaining demesne m- armite will increase as time amnion. The servant problem would be comparatively easy to solve in numberless households, if there was no cook- ing and washing up, and from time to time we hear of schemes to supply well-cooked meals from a common centre. This plan has been tried in the neighbourbreol of flats, and the "London Distributing Kitchens. Ltd.," is the name of an undertaking that caters more generally for the public at large. It supplies dinner for 2s. lunch for is 6J, and breakfast for is, or for a family at £1 per head per week. The cloth and everything for the table , Is supplied with the meal, which is delivered by motor ' ear in specially prepared baskets, everything being kept quite hot. It is therefore almost like living at in hotel, as far as the meals are concerned and the food and cook- ing are of the test. Theoretically the plan offers many advantages, and people ought to be able to live better by it, just as on the same principle, it costs less per head to keep a family of 10 or 12, than it does a family of three or four. Whether the public will support what is really • ready cooked meal factory is another matter, but it will be interesting to watch the result of these attempts to introduce a great social change in London. MADGE.

\ Warrington Corporation Officials' Trip. About 40 of the officials and staff of the Corpora- tion, with friends, had a trip to Llangollen on Saturday. After breaking the journey at Chester, the party reached their destination early in tile afternoon. On arrival they were met by convey- ances, and, starting from the Royal Hotel, had a most pleasant drive to the aqueduct, the scenery in the district being greatly admired. On returning to the Royal Hotel the party sat down to an excel- lent tee. The meal over, the visitors split up into Fections, and visited the various places of interest in the locality. The party left Llangollen for War- rington at 7.30 and arrived back about half-past nine, after having Spent a most enjoyable day. The management for the trip gave general satisfaction and were carried out by the following committee : Messrs Brierley. Lawrence, Houghton (secretary) Crank and Hind.

The fight on the corn tax will be renewed on the schedule of the Finance Bill. When that is reachPd (sayA the Lobby correspondent a the "Standard ") proposals will be made for the exclusion from the duty of nearly every article named in it, and the discussions will be prolonged by the opposition as much as possible, A great effort is to be made to get maize exempted from the duty, and the pressure from both sides of the Hcuss In favour of this course will be considerable.

THE EXAMINER FRIDA Y JUNE 13. 1902

than, as ne turnecr to centmu.e nrs wauc, lie reeled up•against the tall, dark-eyed captain oi his regiment. rarcion me, mon capitaine, I ask a thousand pardons," the young fellow exclaimed. "I hope

"Never mind," interrupted the captain, "though you nearly spun me out into the middli. of the street. H(.5 I mon dieu ! your head has the strength of an ancient battering ram; come along with me, Loigerot, come, I say, Lam out of temper, and you can usually smooth me down, my friend."

Captain Guiney was older and more cultivated than the lieutenant, but the two men had been friends ever since the young soldier, before he rose from the ranks, had saved his officer's life by risking his own. Captain Guiney was a cynic and a pessimist, and the honest simplicity of his brave little friend was, he often told himself, his best preventive against misanthropy. Loigerot laughed tilbthe tears came. "Pardon me, my friend," he said at last, making an effort to check his rather rolling gait, "but I cannot help laughing. I say temyself, here is a man with everything to make him happy, and he spends his time in :making troubles." He laughed again. Captain Guiney shrugged his shoulders and curled his moustache round a finger.

"You are daring," he—answered, "but I admit that what you say may be sometimes justly applied to me, not, however, in the present case : I am vexed with the folly of our comrade, La Peyre. He has actually engaged himself to that pretty little girl, Mademoiselle Sophie Devisme, and when .he gets his captaincy, he proposes to leave the army; did you ever know such folly ? " Loigerot was silent, and his friend looked sharply at him.

"I ask you as a man of sense, Loigerot ; did you ever hear of such folly?"

The lieutenant's clieeks had become a fiery red, and as he answered, he puffed out his words with extra emphasis. "Ma foi, it does not seem to me folly, if they love one another, and—aw—have enough to live 0n.,,

Captain Guiney gave his companion a suspicious glance as if he doubted his sanity, then, with un- disguised wonder, he looked the stout little officer well overofrom the top of his cap to the hem of his trousers. .

"Good heavens I " he said, as much to himself as o Loigerot, "is it possible that a fellow who has so distinguished himself can utter such a senti- ment? Why, my excellent Achille, we are no longer in the Middle Ages. What can love have to do with marriage, I ask you ? " Loigerot laughed loudly. "Well. well, ' he said as soon as he could speak, "you seem in a bad skin, Gainey; I do not say that every married man loves his wife, or that every wife loves her husband, but surely life must be happier when this is so; I hope to marry one of these days, captain, and I certainly mean to love my wife and to be loved by her in return." The Captain looked extremely serious; he knew that Loigerot seldom spoke of himself, or of his feelings: he had heard some gossip about a hand- some young widow, but it had not occurred to him till to-day, that there was anything more than a flirtation between this lady and his yoang friend. "When are you going to see your _property, Loigerot?" he asked abruptly. "It is, I fancy, a rather long journey from Rouen to Strasbourg ? " "Aha," the lieutenant laughed again, and rolled from side to side in his delight, "yes, yes, that is a good joke, my property indeed! Mon Dieu, time enough for me to take it in hand when I leave the army. No my friend, I need not go to Strasbourg; the property is let to a trustworthy tenant, who pays the rents regularly into the bank, where—aw —where for the present I am content to let them assimilate."

"Lucky dog," the captain said, "to be sure of promotion, and to be left an income at your age, by a cousin you never saw; these are things that do not fall to the lot of everyone. That young fool, La Peyre, is not half as well off as you are; I wish he had some of your good sense ; come, let us go to the cafe."

THE BLIND BEGGAR,

BY KATHARINE S. MACQUOID,

(Author of "Patty," " Appledore Farm," &c.)

===== 0002601_19020613_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
PART L --CHAPTER I.

The sun was shining on the long, semi.circular flight of steps in front of the Church of St. Maclou ; the carved tracery of the lovely portal showed perfectly against the rich shadow within the porch; the breadth of light, however, invaded part of this shadow, and fell notabiy on the white cap of an old beggar-woman who sat by the baize door of the church.

She was dressed in black and sombre grey; she was not ragged and dirty, like some of the Rotten church beggars, but it was easy to see that she was blind.

She raised her head at the sound of footsteps, and holding out her right hand, she slowly chinked her money box. It was such a pathetic little think, seemingly made by one miserable centime against the metal box. The beggar-woman smiled when the footsteps paused. "Pardon me, madame," said a voice from the bottom of the steps, "I see a friend in the porch, there."

The words were puffed out, and sounded pompous, while the solemn expression on the speaker's round, rather flat face, fitted the import- &nce of his manner; there was, however, a humorous twinkle in his small dark eyes. He went up the steps, dropped a franc in the beggar's box, and said gently, How do you find yourself to-day, mother, eh?"

The answer was not ;whined out in the tone of a professional beggar. "May God bless and reward you, my officer. I find myself as usual, many thanks." Lieutenant Loigerot looked puzzled and some- what alarmed. Someone had told him that Elise Carron the beggar of St. Maclou was a witch, and he had not believed the story • he looked seriously at his rather portly figure, and his tightly-buttoned blue military frock, crimson silk sash, and flaming red trousers, —he was sure that no part of his uniform could have touched the beggar. How could she, then, have guessed his profession, unless, indeed, his sword had clanked as he mounted the steps? "My friend," he spoke very kindly, "I wish you would tell me why you have twice called me officer? The last time I had the pleasure of speaking to you, and to-day ?" The beggar smiled, but she turned her face towards the street; she had been so long blind that her hearing was singularly acute, and she had become aware of a sound unheard by the lieutenant. This was the frou-frou of a silk parasol, as the lovely lady who stood waiting for the lieutenant moved it impatiently above her dainty bonnet. "My secret is soon told, monsieur," the beggar- woman answered. "For some weeks past you have never passed me by unnoticed, and when I asked who my generous benefactor was, there were plenty to tell me "he is Lieutenant Loigerot of the 75th regiment, now stationed at Rouen, one of the bravest officers of his regiment." Loigerot reddened with pleasure. "I must not detain monsieur from the lady who waits for him below," the beggar added. "How the devil "—(the lieutenant checked himself)—" An revoir, mother, take care of your- self."

"One thousand devils," he thought, as he went down the steps, "she must be a witch, or else her blindness is a sham; how else in the name of wonder can she know that Madame Jamain is waiting for me?" This wonder so oppressed the brave lieutenant, that he forgot to smile at the handsome lady with the gay parasol. Madame Jamain was very handsome. She had the fair flower-like complexion which seems to be the distinct prerogative of youth, her skin was smooth and satiny, her forehead clear and white, her eyebrows were fine and well-marked, and her large blue eyes were full of light and colour: perhaps her high-bridged nose and her lips were a trifle thin, but when her lips parted they shewed a gleam of dainty little teeth; her fuzz of fair hair was shining and silky, and the pose of her head was full of distinction; Madame Jamain's figure was well-proportioned, though perhaps rather full for her youthful appearance. Her air of distinction had quickly captivated Lieutenant Loigerot. That this adorable woman, who moved like a queen, should smile on him, should even accept his escort to the door of her house, was still, after a fortnight's acquaintance, more like a wonderful dream to him than a reality: he had made her acquaintance one evening when she was leaving the theatre, and her carriage was not to be found. The fair widow now said, "Does monsieur find the old beggar so very interesting ?" Her little smile made Loigerot nervous: he feared she was covertly laughing at him. " Ma foi, madame," and he reddened underthe full gaze of those blue eyes. "Devil take it," he thought, "how tender they could look if they :those." Then aloud, "I go to see the old woman, madame, because—because—(he began to stammer, and no longer puffed out his words), "it is, madame, a souvenir; the old beggar's voice reminds me of my mother's." His companion smiled and showed her pretty teeth.

i "Monsieur s modest about his good deeds. I have noticed that old woman sitting there, and she does not move my pity. Mon dieu, I believe she is as well off as I am, but monsieur will permit me to say that I think his mother would scarcely feel complimented by such a likeness." She shifted her parasol to get a better view of her admirer; he was tugging at his moustache as if he expected to extract a suitable answer from its coarse tawny hair. Loigerot was congratulating himself that le had not said how much his mother had resembled the blind beggar in her poverty, except that his mother had earned her living by her own hard work, instead of earning it by begging: when he had been drawn for the army, his mother had insisted on his joining instead of finding him a substitute, because she had not means to help him on in any other walk of life. Loigerot was very proud of his mother, but her memory was sacred, locked up in a corner of the young fellow's heart; he could not talk about her even to Madame Jamain.

"Pardon me, mafilame, the likeness is in the voice." Then he added sadly, "My mother died while I was at Solferino."

His companion's blue eyed glistened with sympathy. "'That was sad, indeed! It must have dimmed the lustre of your honours, for you, monsieur, greatly distinguished yourself in the famous battle." She glanced approvingly at his decoration, while he reddened, and held himself more erect than ever. He pushed up his stubby imperial with his stumpy fingers, and smiled till his eyes looked like dark lines. He could not at once think of a suitable acknowledgment of this compliment, but at last he cleared his throat, raised his cap, and looked more beaming man ever. " Madame—aw—madame i, oc. kind. I don't know—aw—how to thank madame sufficiently, but I love my profession. Yes, madame, it is my glory and my joy to be a soldier." Madame Jamain smiled, then she sighed. "I cannot wonder, when you are such an orna- ment to your profession. But,' monsieur, I greatly regret that I can no longer listen to you; some day you will tell me about your military adventures, will you not? I shall delight in listening to the story of a real hero." Her eyes were eloquent, and they bewitched him. He seemed to tread on air, while he won- dered how he could contrive a longer interview with this lovely lady. He had hoped than when they reached her door to-day, she would ask him to come in, yet he could not but admire her conven- tional propriety. A bright idea suddenly came to bin,. _ _ _ _

"Madame,' he puffed out, "it would be most condescending if you would, next Sunday, accept my escort to Pa Pouille ; in such fine weather even you, madame, might find the excursion pleasant." Madame Jamain's face had been screened by her parasol, she moved it and looked rosy from tiro reflection ; she smiled sweetly as she aßswered : "Willingly, monsieur, I have often wished lie visit La Pouille. I shall be charmed to accompany monsieur on Sunday." They had reached her house, and she held out her land; the lieutenant bowed over her dainty lemon-coloured glove; his face glowed with happi- ness. "I will then say adieu, till Sunday, madame. I— sw—shall have the honour of calling a quarter before three o'clock, aw—if that hour will suit the we- venience of madame." Perfectly, monsieur." With another very graceful bow, Madame Jamain vanished somewhat abruptly in the now open doorway. Leizerot stood lookinu low2inuir after her; and

MISS PRISM'S ANARCHIST.

Adapted from the French lai H. M. Ashton,

With her bag of needlework in her hand and the basket containing her dog Fido under her arm Miss Prism got into the train. The carriage was empty and she made herself comfortable, feeling thankful that she was going to have it all to herself with her darling Fido, when at the last moment a man opened the door and took his seat in the carriage. Miss Priscilla Prism had an instinctive horror of the male sex, no man having ever paid homage to her charms. When she reached the age of thirty Miss Prism had made a solemn and obstinate vow of celi- bacy, and since that time a considerable number of years had passed. Her life was very simple and mapped out by rules she never deviated from. Her only companion was Fido, her Fido for whom she cherished a boundless affection. And if every year she spent the winter in a sheltered valley of the Tyrol, from which she was re- turning to day to England—it was as much for the good of the dog as for herself. As soon as the unknown climbed into the carriage,Miss Prism thought of getting out and finding another compartment but the train was already in motion so she sank back into her corner, pressing Fido to her heart. Over her spectacles Miss Prism stealthily examined her fellow traveller whose face and dress made her feel uneasy. His complexion was bilious and his beard black and bushy. He had a travelling bag, an uinbrella,and carried in his left hand a round object enveloped in a red handkerchief. This certainly could be no ordinary package. It must con- ceal something important and mysterious; that was plainly to be seen by the precaution and the infinite care with which its bearer handled it. He put his bag on the seat in front of him and his umbrella beside it, but he was a long time in deciding where to place the red package, but finally placed it by his side on the cushion. He brooded over this red packet with a singular intentness, murmuring strange words such as "my little treasure," " the great day has come " and We are going to have a word with the King." Miss Prism felt somewhat alarmed, perhaps he was a madman. What could he wish to say to his Icing ? What were the contents of the package which he called kis treasure ?

Then the man, entirely absorbed in himself, drew a cigar from his pocket and began to light it. To smoke before a woman and without 'asking her permission ! To smoke in Fido's compartment I For Fido could not bear tobacco smoke, it made him sneeze till he was ill. Besides he had bad his breakfast just before starting and his digestion would be upset. Miss Prism rose from her corner. " Sir.',she said, " this is not ,a smoking compart- ment ! '

The an started, and looking with confusion at Miss Prism he said in a disappoiuted tone " Pardon,madam. I thought I was alone."

It was eVident that the man was very vexed at not being alone. He didn't want to be looked at or recog- nised. This was extremely suspicious. The unknown put his cigar back into his pocket, Miss Prism retired into her corner in hostile and inquisitive silence, not losing sight of a single movement or gesture of her mysterious fellow traveller. At the frontier, the train stopped, and when the customs officers had gone through the carriage inspecting the baggage,Miss Prism with a sigh of relief noticed that the man had dis- appeared. Where had he got to ? The train had already started when a man hastily opened the door, climbed into the carriage and sank down on the seat. It was the bad man again ! He still carried as carefully as possible the red package. The explanation seemed very simple to Miss Prism. This package doubtless contained some contraband article. The man had dis- appeared at the approach of the officers and reappeared after their departure. He must be a very dangerous person. _

Miss Prism's fears grew more and more alarming. She noticed that at each station gendarmes in unusual numbers were on duty on the platform, and examined every traveller that 'got into the train. This did not re-assure her. Miss Prism was determined to find out the reason of it, and stopping the guard as he passed their compartment, she asked him if there was any special reason for this extra precaution. "It is on account of the anarchists madam " replied he. " The King of Carpentaria passes here to-day on his way to Weltersbad, and the police have got wind of a plot against his life," and he went away. A fearful thought at once flashed through Miss Prism's mind, she turned pale, a second had sufficed for her to understand every- thing. This man of peculiar manners and strange dress who sought solitude and fled from custom house officers —he was an anarchist ! The object that he held with so much care, from which he would not be separated; that he called his treasure, that was going to say a word, an atrocious word, to the King—it was a bomb ! Yes it was all clear enough. Msss Prism had in her compartment an anarchist, carrying a bomb destined to kill the King of Carpentaria. She, Miss Prism was travelling with an assassin ! What should she do ? Pull the alarm cord ? Xiss Prism was in that state of mind in which the simplest things are those which one least dreams of, whilst one finds oneself capable of doing the most extravagant things. Suddenly, by a chance in which ' she saw the band of Providence, she noticed that the anarchist was asleep. At her side poor Fido slept peacefully in his basket. Miss Prism gave her little pet a farewell kiss, then gathering all her courage she crept towards the anarchist and leaned over the round object wrapped in a red handkerchief. She drew back I terrified. This bomb was more than a bomb,it was an infernal machine! She could hear a light tic-tae from the inside. No doubt the jolting of the train had set in motion the clockwork which regulated thelime of the explosion. There was not a minute to lose. With a swift movement Miss Prism seized the machine. The only window open was the one by which she had been seated. It seemed as if years passad while she crossed the comparanent. The train at this moment crossed a little brook which owing to the recent rains had over- flowed into adjoining meadow, so much so that the water came almost to the level of the railway. Miss Prism rased her arm to throw the odious engine as far las possible into the water. Suddenly she heard a shout behind her, the anarchist had awakened! As quick as lightning Miss Prism threw the bomb with all her strength. It fell into the water with a great splash Then she turned towards the anarchist.

.- " Curse you" cried he, seizing her violently by the shoulders, What have you done." Miss Prism felt herself lost. Exalted by ?he feelins of danger, she looked bravely at him and with the accent of a martyr said "I have saved the king! have destroyed your bomb " "Destroyed my bomb? Saved the king ?"—repeated the anarchist, as if lie was trying to understand her, then he shouted " get out of my way ! " The excitement had been too much for Miss Prism, all her strength and presence of mind had evaporated, she collapsed entirely and was completely in the power of the anarchist. This man certainly bad the most inconceivable effrontery. He who feared to be recog- nised or seen, did not hesitate to rush to the alarm signal, and pull the handle furiously. The train stopped. The guard appeared at the door and asked what had happened. " My wife is ill " said the anarchist, indicating Miss Prism. "She cannot remain in this carriage without great danger. _Let us get out." . . .

She, Miss Prism, married And married to an anarchist ! _ Oh, the horror of it !

The guard had no .objection. He brought out the bag and the umbrella while the anarchist raised Miss Prism in his arms, half-dead with horror and shame. The train proceeded on its way. The anarchist had placed the unfortunate Miss Prism on the ground. "Now " said he in a menacing voice " Since you have thrown away—my bomb—you are goineto help me find it."

Miss Prism did not dream of resisting. "What shall we do P ' she murmured. . _ _

"What shall we do.? 'iiigqi7a7;i't;stion. I'll tell you what we are going to do. I, am going to smoke a cigar. roe are going into the water to look for my bomb. You will take off your shoes and stocking and you'll paddle about in it until you have found it ! " This time Miss Prim rebelled To die, she was resigned, but take off her shoes and stockings before this man, she would not ; modesty which had always been Miss Prism's dominating virtue forbade it. Even when she was a little girl Miss Prism would not have committed such an indiscretion. Not for worlds would she obey the order of this miserable wretch. So Miss Prism went into the, water with her shoes on. The water was excessively cold, she felt it coming through the soles of her shoes and it was a most disagree- able experience. How long would she be searching over her spectacles for some trace of the red handker- I chief. It seemed to her as if she had already been an age in the water and while she paddled about her I prosecutor sat on the fence smoking his cigar and star- ing at her ferociously. "Sir" she cried "I have found it, will you come and take it ?" "Bring it yourself!" Was the reply of her inexorable tormentor.

She had to do it ! so she seized the packaze by the knot in the handkerchief and chilled with cold and fear she regained the bank, not without making a great effort to preserve her dignity. The man snatched it from her hands, stared at her for the last time, burst into a roar of laughter and ran away as quickly as he could.

Miss Prism stood transfixed and could not understand

The way to sell goods is to humour people. The money you pay. for Fels-Naptha soap is still yours if you choose to ask your grocer for it, 2,1,d a bar.

Fels-Naptha soap Peninsular House Monument London EC

- 4

why or how she still lived. However when she had assured herself that all danger was passed, she pulled herself together, and then a terrible thought presented itself. This crime that she had tried to prevent, this crime would be committed. The anarchist had gone,he would kill the king—unless he was overtaken. Yes, she must do it at all costs, she must arrive before the anarchist.

Miss Prism walked to the nearest station and took the next train to Weltersbad. Scarcely had she got out when she asked " Whore is the king ? ' She was told that the king had indeed arrived, but there was no chance at all of her getting near him. In tho station transformed into a reception hall, he received from the town officials the welcome which is the prerogative of crowned heads. With the exception of a few special people who had obtained the favour of an audience,entrance to the hall was strictly forbidden. The crowd massed around could only assist in the ceremony from afar by punctuating the congratulatory addresses with hurrahs.

The time was well chosen for congratulations, when any minute might be the last of the king's life ! Taking no notice of the shouting and pushing Miss Prism forced her way through the crowd, got through the line of guards and found herself in the hall where the privileged ones were waiting to have audience with the king. What a spectacle presented itself to Miss Prism, frightened but triumphant! That man who was bow- ing before the king she recognized,she would know him among a hundred! It was he! There was no mis- taking him. The same bilious complexion, the same thick black beard, the same look ! Another detail dissipated all doubts, from his pocket hung the end of a red handkerchief. Miss Prism had come in time. She stretched out towards the man her avenging arm and in a loud voice cried— " Arrest him, he is an anarchist !" To her great astonishment the words created no stir, but provoked great laughter. And the man turned round and she heard these words: "Sire, behold the foolish woman of whom I had the honour to speak to you It was she who threw into the water the clock made for your Majesty. The clock is so perfect that neither the jolting nor the bath have done any damage to its mechanism -- ..

And he -showed to the King with pride,all the marvels that this clock could perform, this clock of which he was the inventor, which he had carried so carefully, and which had inspired Miss Prism with fears so exaggera- ted !

Motor Mail Breakdown.

There was a broakflown on -Monday in the Ilan- chester-Liverpool motor mail service. The Liver- pool van had scarcely got clear of Victoria-streat when, at the foot of the hill in St. John's-lane, jr stopped through the fracture of the axle of the rear wheels, and remained there all night and far into the dayliht yesterday. The parcels were immedi- ately transferred to one oE the old horse-drawn van., and arrived in Manchester in good time for he first delivery on Tnesday morning.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

Free Church Protest at Warrington.

Speeches by Dr. Monro Gibson and Mr. Cameron, M.P.

A well-attended meeting, convened by the War- rington and District Free Church Council was held in the Parr Hall,Warrington,on Tuesday evening, to protest against the Education Bill. The chair was occupied by Mr C E Parker, president of the Free Church Council. There were also present :—The Rev J Monro Gibson, D.D of London ; Mr R Cameron, M.P, ; the Revs John Yonge, E T Reed, C Finch, J L Nlilne, John Davies, T Williams and C H Hocken (secretary); Councillors F B Roberts, James Evans, Brough, Peacock, 'Ciunion, Ellison, William Pierpoint and Barton ; Messrs E .1 Guest, 3,P, H E Garnett, Thomas Birchall, J. MR ARMITAGE AND THE CLERICAL PARI T. Mr Armitage, in the course of his letter sail : " I should welcome a truly national system of education, which, while dealing generously with the denominational schools, extended and strengthened the power of the School Boards ; but this Bill I regard as an unfair attempt of the clerical party to strengthen tha Established Church. which is the Caurch of the minority, at the expense of the ratepayers and of the Free Churches, whose adherents are the great ms3ority of the religious British people. (Applause). I object to the transfer to the sectarian managers of the power of c mtrol over the public schools, which are sustained by Imperial taxes and local rates, from the people who pay the rates and taxes. Under this Bill, if it becomes law, Sacerdotalism and Roman Catholicism will ba taught at the public expense ; the compromise of 1870, to which the Free Churches have bean loysi, is abrogated, the in- iCe to which Nonconformi its in the villages are exposed is aggravated and intensified, and the dying embers of sectarian strife, all over the land, are re-lighted and embittered. I cannot, believe that the vast majority of the English people will allow the clivical party to gain its desire to get fuller control than they have already over the edu- cation of the children of the people." (Applause.) The Church of England on the whole had opposed the cause of national education. When a bill passed the House of Cerninens enabling rateeayers in each parish to set no a parish school, it was throws out of the House of Lords by the Archbishop of Canterbury bedeues it was not for the interests of the Church. They hold aloof and put their foot on the movement. But when they saw it was going to be a success they decided to establish separate schools of their own. They were the Schismatics. They were not turned out, they went out and established a separate thing of their owe and called it the National Society for Proutotiug the E location of the Poor it. the Principles of the Es- tablished Church. (Applause). That was what he (Dr Gib See) celled generosity. (Hear, hear). Why would net the Church of England trustithe people ?If the Established Church— what right had it to be established only in so far as it was the church of the people ? If it Was the church of the people why Was it afraid to trust the people in the matter of education? Phe Presbyterians were not.the Con- gregationalists were not, neither were the Wesleyaes or the other Noneenformist bodies. The clsrgy could not stand it, they could not rejoice in it and be glad that the children of working men had at last an education that had never been given in this country before except through the Board Sehools. That was the position of things in the main, and they wore determined to capture the children at the public ex penee and to putetbeir own schools on a level with the Board Schools as far as finances ware concerned. That was the real origin of the Bill. (Applause.) He did not think the Goverement would have brought forward the Bill if the clergy had not been behind them. Ten thousand petitions were sent in the month of November to Mr Self our to push on the measure. How eager they were to make hay while the E un shone! What did the Bill propose to do ? aplenty Councils were the new authotitien. Tee School Boards were to be dis- solved, dethroned. Wier ? Because they were too sue- cessfal. There was no other reason that ceuld possibly be given. For hie part if he had an authority to select he would select those whe hod an experience in educa- tional matters, and who had proved by their success: that they knew how to manage education work. Coun- cils could not be experts in education bet apprentices. The Government had made a gigantic mistake in dismissing the Sehool Boards. The committee to whom would be delegated the work of education would not be respons:ble to the County Ceuceil that created it. He contended that every com- mittee should be responsible to the Council. The com- mittee would not he directly elected by the people and a large proportion of the members would beoutside gentle- men who might have a knowledge of or a taste for educational matters or might not. Fla held that such a committee was not qualified to superintend the educa- tion of a whole county or a big borough, and to superintend secondary education is part. Members of county and borough councils had already enough to do. If they had a Board:compoeed entirely of educationalists and experts they would have enough to do. Education Iva.] worth a board for itself. (Applause.) Under the Bill, the power of appointing teachere belonged to the old managers, and the placer of teaohing what dogmas they pleased was theirs. He did not believe in sacerdotal theories ; he did not believe a mail had a right to call kimaelf a priest. He did not think a man had tho right to assume that he was superior to any one else, except in character, service, and sacrifice. If , he was superior in that respect, he (Mr Cameron) honoured him, but if he assumed the title of priest officially and used it purely as an external title, he for one, denied the theory, and every Norma- formiet denied it. At least 700,000 children of Dissentere attended church schools. Were they excused tey the conscience clause? Were they sent into a separate room? Here was the testimony of one of the Diocesan inspectors of schools, and when he (the speaker) read it he was aetonished. The Inspector said

_ - - " Our syllabus is always arranged so as to tive distinctive denominational instruction. I always saw it was given w'-en I was diocesan inspector, and always asked the children, chiefly the children of Nonconformist parents, questions bearing upoa it. This is the case throughout Lincolnshire. Thus, in fact, we're training the children of Nonconformists to be children of the Church." Dissenters had borne this for a quarter of a century,and he thought it was timl they told the Church party that they would not bear it any longer. (Applause.) He contended that for church people to say they were offeaded at having to pgy a School Board rate was absurd, as they could: make use of the schools like anyone else. If the clergy wanted dogmas taught let them preach them and teach them. and become heroes and martyrs in their propagation,but the meanest thing they could do was to ask a mass of the popelation to pay for it who did not believe in it. (Hear, hear.) They wanted the breath of popular opinion, an eye upon public men and a scrotinv as to how the money was to he speet. He knew of no higher method of government in this world than the will of the people. They could do smonthing towards mending or ending this Bill. Tne most effective ;method was to write to toeir member personally. He knew their member, and be thought he would be amenable if he found that the majority of the people of this great borough were against the Bill because they believed in education more than the clergy, and that he would either abstain from voting or would votes; they wished. (Applause.)

The Rev. Morley Stevenson and the Religious Difference.

Special eervices in aid of the day schools were held at St. James Church, Latchford, on Sunday. In the morning the preacher was the Rev M)rley Stevenson, Principal of the Training College, who took as his text : " And Jesus increased in wisdom and stature, and in favour with God and man." The preacher said a churce without a School was sadly handicapped, for the achool was the nursery in which the principles of church teach- ing must be given if they were to b 3 put in practice in later life. The scho els of the parish were excellent in every way ;in buildiog, in staff and in results, as was shown by the receet report In the Parish Magazine. Such a work deserved the help of all who had it in their power to help. Turning from the subject of these particular schools, be passed to the Iconsideration of the education question, which he de- fined as a perfecting of the whole nature—body soul and /Title The text, gave them the key to the whole question. Christ had left the pattern in that as in every- thing else. Ile increased "iu stature ;" the body grew and was perfected ; "in wisdom " She intellectual powers were developed ; and the spiritual part of His nature grew so as ta be "in favour with God sod man." That threefold education was what was aimed at in school% Teachers studied the laws of health and tried to apply them in dealing with trig children. The build- ings were made as perfect as possible from • sanitary I point of view. All care was taken to train and develop the mind, and every good teacher strove to educate the highest part of the nature---his soul—ind to in ake kin honeet,true,temperate and God-fearing. The future of education in this country was causing great anxiety at present. The feeling about the Education Bill was very strong. Many were opposed to it, and it was greatly to be hoped that the Churchpeople, if they entered into the controversy, would approach the sub- ject with self-control, patience.. and justice. By that means many of the difficulties might be removed. There were two great principles to be contended for which must not be given up. Toe first was religious education in which they demanded the right to bring up a child in the religious principles of this parent, and while ready to extend t 3 others what they clainasd for themselves, must be prepared to stand up for that principle through all diffieultiee ; secondly, their share of the public money. Objectors to the Bill said that it was not fair to help the Church out of rate- payers' money. Two-thirds of the rates of this country came out of the pockets of Church people, and they asserted their right to a share. Though much more might be said on the subject, be thought the pulpit was the place for spiritual teaching, rather than for dealing with political matters, so he would conclude with one lesson which all might draw from the text he had chosen. They were all children In one sense, and their life here was a his icing for eternity. They must make the most of the school-times and strive to grow —physi- cally, mentally, and spiritually—keeping their bodies in health to do God's work, aid using for His glory and the good of others the mind Ile had given, and living the spiritual life of Elia children, grow,se, " in favour with God and man."—The preacher at the evening ser- vice was the Rev G B Thurston, rector of Lymm. Special psalms and children's hymns were sung. Mr F Bectb presided at she organ.

THE FREE CHITRCIIES AND THE EDUCATION BILL.

INTERVIEW WITH THE REV. THOMAS LAW.

• The National Free Church Council, of which the Rev. Thomas Law is General Secretary, has taken a foremost place in the a:dtation against the Government's Education Bill. At several of its annual meetings the Council has stated its position on the Education Question— universal School Boards in areas of sufficient magnitude, and no public money with- out public Control. The Bill now before the House of Commons proposes the destruc- tion of School Boards and the support of sectarian schools from the rates without any real or effective contrel by the ratepayers. The Free Churches are up in arms against a Bill embodying these and other obnoxious principles; and the National Council, repre- senting as it does 800 local Councils' with which are affiliated all the Evangelical Free Church denominations of England and Wales, has, naturally, taken its place at the head of the opposition against the Bill. Desiring full information of the operations and plans of the Council, I grasped the opportunity of a short conversation with the Rev. Thomas Law. ' "What is the latest action of the National Council against the Bill ?" I asked Mr. Law. "I may tell you at once," he remarked, " that I have just received a letter from Mr. A. J. Balfour, in which he says he will be happy to receive a deputation of the National Council on the Education Bill. The interview will take place at the House of Commons. I cannot give you the date yet. Mr. Balfour has suggested the 12th of June, but he is willing to consult the eonvenience of the Committee. You know we leave already waited upon the Leader of the Opposition in the House of Commons. We shall present before the Leader of the House of Commons a united front of opposition to the Bill."

"Are the Councils and Churches taking up the matter thoroughly?" "Yes, with the greatest earnestness and en- thusiasm. I see my friend, Dr. Clifford, has been saying that the country has never been so deeply stirred within his recollection. That is my experience, too. Letters are pouring into my office at the Memorial Hall by every post from all parts of the country, containing strong resolutions by local Councils and Churches, and proving that they are rousing i their neighbourhoods n the most effective fashion. What has greatly surprised one, too, is that many organisations not directly con- nected with the National Council are sending us their resolutions and telling us what steps they are taking against the Bill Innumerable suggestions are being received at the Central Office from'all parts of the country as to methods of conducting the campaign. All these are carefully considered, and, where practicable, adopted. The prominent leaders of the Free Churches are devoting all their time, apart from their Church work, to this great fight, and have placed themselves at the disposal of the National Council for meetings in any part of the country. Among ministers there are such men as Revs. Dr. .Clifford, R. J. Campbell, M.A., J. Hirst Hollowell, Dr. Aked, J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., Dr. Horton, Silas K. Hocking, C. Silvester Horne, MA., Dr. Townsend, Dr. Monro Gibson, Dr. Meharry, J. D. Jones, M.A., 8.D., Dr. Mackennal, Charles H. Kelly, Bernard J. Snell, J. H. Jowett, M.A., W. Cuff, and many others. I need hardly say that our leadinglaymen are heart and soul with us and meetings all over England are being addressed by the following, awing others: Messrs. R. W. Perks, M.P., George White, M.P., A. E. Hutton, M.P., W. S. Caine, M.P., Henry Broadhurst, M.P., Lloyd George, M.P., J. Compton Rickett, M.P., Dr. Massie, Percy W. Bunting, M.A., J. Carvell Williams, Albert Spicer, Robert Whyte, and Alderman Duckworth. I am myself visit- ing every part of the Kingdom and can testify that everywhere a great tide is rising." "What steps are the Councils taking besides holding public meetings ?" "Well,' replied Mr. Law, "besides the central meetings they are holding gatherings all over their areas. For instance, the Northum- berland and Durham Federation has held or arranged for 100 meetings. Leeds Council, too' has organised sixty meetings against the Bill. i And the campaign s being conducted on the same scale all over the country. Many of the town Councils are holding meetings in every church, and the rural Councils are carrying the agitation into every village, and in nearly every case crowds assemble, even when there are no speakers from a distance. The man in the street and the working man are thoroughly awake. Indeed, we have organised quite a number of meetings purely for working men, and have supplied speakers for them. Nothing could be more wide of the mark than to suggest that this is a manufactured agitation. The fact is that our great difficulty is to keep pace with the country." "Yes," continued Mr. Law, in answer to a question, "the campaign is imposing an enor- mous burden of correspondence upon the central office, but the work is of the utmost im- portance. We perceived from the first how serious was the situation created by the Bill, and at very short notice we organised the great con- ference of Free Church Councils which was held at St. James's Hall on April 15. One cannot too strongly emphasise the representative character of that gathering. It was something to crowd St. James's Hall at a week-day after- noon, but our assembly was a great deal more than a public meeting. Each of the representa- tives brought a mandate from his local Council with him. The Councils in all parts of England had sent their chosen leaders to utter their voices in righteous condemnation of an attempt to place the education of the people in the hands of the priests and to make the whole nation pay towards the teaching of dogmas which half the nation rejects. The influence of that gathering has been felt ever since in every part of the land." "That conference took a very strong and straight line r "Yes, it did," replied Mr. Law, emphatically, "and one of the strongest points in the resolu- tions was that the revolutionary changes in the Bill were not submitted to the country for approval at the last General Election, and that, consequently, the present Parliament holds no -mandatls from the people to deal with educa- tion. A speaker at a public meeting the other day said: • I voted for the Government at the last election because of my views on the war. I am utterly opposed to the Education Bill. Many of you,' he said to his audience, are in I the same case. You supported the Govern- ment candidate because you believed that the war was just and necessary. You never dreamed that the educational system of the country was to be overturned, and you hate the present Bill. You must write and tell your member that this feeling is very strong every- where."

"And how did you proceed after the St. James's Hall conference ?" I asked Mr. Law. "We have been engaged without ceasing since April 15 in the work of agitating and educating. We sent out a circular to all our local Councils, giving them hints and suggestions, which have been universally followed—public meetings, open-air meetings, letters to members of Parlia- ment, village meetings, and the passing of reso- lutions at Church meetings—these were among the methods of work suggested. And just a few days ago we sent a letter to every Free Chunk 'there 22 Beemlity

,

minister in England, strongly urging him to bring the proposals of the Government before his congregation at the earliest possible date, either at a Sunday service or a special meeting of the members."

"I believe, too, you have distributed a consider. able quantity of literature against the Bill." "Ah, yes," said Mr. Law, "we have already distributed gratis millions of our paw- phlets and leaflets throughout the country. That has been a very heavy, serious drain on our esources. First of all, we printed an account of the St. James's Hall Conference together with the adopted resolutions. Besides that, we have issued eight leaflets. Dr. Town- send, our President, has written on How to defeat the Education Bill,' Prof. J. S. Banks, the President-elect of the Wesleyan Conference, on The Duty of the Nation,' Mr. George White, M.P., the Vice-President of the Baptist Union, on 'An Unwarranted Attack on Free Church- men,' Dr. Clifford on Clericalism and the Edu- cation-Bill,' and the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., on The Education Bill and Secondary Education.' Other leaflets are entitled, 'The Education Bill: A Clear Summary of its Pro- visions,'Endowing the Parson's Schools,' and Reasons for Ending the Education Bill.' These leaflets are going out in immense quanti- ties daily. A large staff is at work at the Central Offices day and night despatching them. The demands for this literature come from all quarters." "You mentioned the drain on the National Council's resources. Does that cause you any anxiety, Mr. Law ?" "Not anxiety, quite. But it is undoubtedly a serious business. We have spent hundreds of pounds in organising and in the distribution of literature, and up to the present this has had to be Met in the main from our own ordinary resources. Ido not think our people fully realise how enormous is the outlay in conducting a campaign of this sort. We have issued an appeal, signed by our officers, which I hope will bring this home to them. We have every confidence that our friends will take care that the success of our campaign is not endangered Ly lack of funds. We have the men, the machinery, the en- thusiasm. All we need is money. Several thousands are wanted immediately. If a hun- dred well-to-do Free Churchmen would each give us £5O or £lOO, and give unit immediately, I should be immensely relieved. Our poorer friends are doing nobly. A lady has just written to me to say that she has no money, but will gladly send on her humble stock of jewellery, a few working men in a country church have sent on Ils. 6d., and a poor working woman has sent half-a-guinea. Oh no ," said Mr. Law, eagerly, "I believe in the generosity of Free Churchmen. When they understand how heavy our outlay is, they will respond to our appeal. But certainly our immediate needs are great." "And if the Bill passes, Mr. Law," I asked, "what then ?"

"Well," replied Mr. Law, thoughtfully, "if the Bill passes we shall be ready. Our:resources are not exhausted. We are prepared for any emergency, and if our friends in the country will realise that to fight their battle costs money, and will keep us supplied, I have not the shadow of a doubt as to the result."

The amount that is immediately needed, having regard to the resources of Free Church- men and the immense importance of the issues at stake, is not large. It is very necessary that while the Bill is being discussed, clause by clause, in the Hume of Commons the work of educating and agitating should Le continued unceasingly throughout the country. It is admitted on all hands that up to the present the National Council has proved adequate to the immense demands made upon it, and what it has been able to do, with its ordinary re- sources, is a promise of what it will accomplish if adequate financial help is forthcoming. Sub- scriptions should be sent to the Chairman of the Finance Committee, Alderman Evan Spicer, J.P., L.C.C. ; or to the General Secretary, Rev. Thomas Law, at the Memorial Hall, Farringdon- street, London, E.C. •

"WHEN MY WIFE'S A WIDOW."

It has never been said of Irishmen that they are lack- ing in courage. On the contrary, their physical ardour not infrequently outruns their discretion; but that was net se in the case ef the man who uttexed the remark- able words at the head of this article. A private in an Irish regiment on active service in the Crimea during the terrible winter of 1854, was urged by his com- rade. to' volunteer for a very dangerous task which meant almcst certain death, and they sought to incline him to their will by telling him of the glory that would ever after attach to his name if be fell. To this the Irishman replied with the quelein : " What's the use of that to me when my wife's a widow ? " Nct much, we fear. It is the duty of every man to prevent his wile from becoming a widow as long as he possibly can. In the stress of modern civilisation the task becomes an increasingly difficult one. so that a man who succeeds in retaining sound health most be accounted wise in his day and generation. " A few Fears ago," writes Mr Wiiliam Blunn, of 24, Rednal. Brant Green, Worcs, " I began to be troubled with indigestion. Everything I ate seemed to become lodged within rce, the weight at my chest and stomach heing almost tmendt:rable. Mr heart palpitated so that I could only draw my breath with diffi- culty, particularly at night, compelling me to rise and walk about for hours together. It was not because I bad taken too much supper, for my appetite bad almost ceased and I ate barely 6UffiCient to keep me alive. My tongue was coated and an unpleasant taste always present in my mouth. Of course, I lost flesh rapidly, and became thin, weak, and sickly-looking. I consulted two doctors, but their treatment failed to benefit me, so that I went from bad to worse. But luckily for me there were people in Rednsl who knew the worth of Mother Seigsl's Curative Syrup, and I was persuaded to- give it a trial. I took two bottles of it, and they did me 60 much good that I abandoned ire use, believing I could then do without it. In this I was too hasty, and a return of my complaint compq!ed me to again resort Ito it. I therefore took four more bottlea, and conld then truthfully assert that Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup bad wrought a lasting cure. My appetite was improved, I cou:d digest my food, enjoy sound sleep, and in a few months gained eleven pounds in weight. If at any time I feel run down, a dose of it never fails to put me right again.

WORKINGMEN'S DWELLINGS. A Flourishing Building Society at Warrington.

The thirty,Tecond annual general meeting of the members of the Warrington Workingmen's Permanent Benefit BuiMin? Sncicty was held in Golborne-street Schoolroom on Monday evening. Mr J W Bouchier was voted to the chair, ard amengst oth.rs present were Msesra J Harding,E Wallington, E Fenton, R Isherwood, J Baxter. .1 B Starkey, R Sutton, W T Sampson, J Skelton, jun., .1 Longland. and P Dalton (secretar3 ). The CHAIRMAN said he was always sorry tbat more members did not attend the annual meeting, but they eould be certain that if the society was going at all badly there would be a larg.r attendance. The small attendance spoke well for the flourishing condition of the society.

===== 0002601_19020613_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANNUAL REPORT.

The annual report was presented, and read as follows: —The managers have pleasure in laying the thirty- second annual statement of accounts before the share- holders. During the year the sum of £1,700 16a 2d hae been received as subscription+ ; £2,102 83 6d for repay- ments and interest ; and £45 12 ii entranee fee‘, fines, Ilse, making the total receipts £3,845 163 8d ; the work- ing expenses being £7o3i Id. Tee withdrawals amount to £1,943 2s lid. The sum of £2,875 has been advances to members.. There are 219 subscriber', holding 28$1 shares ; and 44 borrowers, holding 1901 shares ; making 263 members, bolding 4791 shares. The bonuses this year amount to £503 2s 61 on the amount lent, of which sum £92 is divided as profit, and £4ll 2s 6d carri, d to the prospective interest account. Under the Building Societies' Act, 1894, the eccounts of all Buildiog Societies are required to be made up in such a form, and to contain such particulars as the chief registrar of friendly societies may, with the approval of the secretary of state, direct. The form of account which has been prescribed by the chief registrar requires that the balance of capital remsining due upou the mortgage securities should be stated separately from the prospective interest thereon ; in other words, the balance of the £B2 183 (tbe nett amount advanced per £100) remaining unpaid is to be distinguished from Use balance of the £l7 103 (the bonus charge) also remain- ing unpaid. The accounts of the society for the past year have been made up on this basis, and work out as follows :—The total amount remaining owing upon wortgaers lie £10,333 be 31, of which only £9,162 10s 111 can be regarded as capital, the balance of £1,170 19s 4d reprEsAnting the prcporlionate amount of interest thereon, calculated for the number of years the several mortgages have still to run. The profits far the year, including £5 131 id from last account, after deducting working expenses, amount to £377 41 61,0ut of which the managers recommend a dividend of 4 per cent, which will take £362 131 101, leavin: a balance of £l4 103 81, which they direct to be carried forward ter next year's account. The whole of the managers retire. Messrs Harding, Wallington, Fenton, Isherwood, Ileuchier, and Baxter are elie,ible, and offer themselves for re-election. Six additional managers will be taken the regiater in rotation. James Harding,ehairman

In commenting upon the report, the Chair-Man called attcnti on to the fact that with the exception of one year the society bad ever since its formation, paid a dividend of four per cent. They would notice that the prospec- tive interest account amounted to £ll7O 193 4.1, which was equal to £ll 103 per cent. (at the total liabilities, and would be sufficient to pay a regular four per cent interest for the whole of the period of nine years and a half which it would take to wind up the Society, if it were found necessary to do so. No society was worked more economically than that society, the total wcrking expenses for the year amounting to only £7O 3e 10s1. One fact wbich he hoped the mernters would bear in mind was that the acc,etv owned no property. There were roavy people who could not understand why a. :building society should not own property, and there ' were, unfortunately many societies owning pro- perty wllich was not always of an eligible character. In that society they sold their money, so to speak, not for speculation, but with the object of en- abling working men to bccome the owners of their houses. The managers made it a rule not to lend money on property outside Warrington, in order that they might be able to inspect the property from time to time. As the managers saw that all property was sufficient security for the money they lent, the society was not likely to have any property coming into its bands. Another satisfactory feature to which he wished to call attention was that the balance at the bank, which last •

year amounted to £1,849, had during the oast year been reduced by half, and the Secretary would tell them that this year the remaining half had already beea absorbed and they bad no balance at the bank, the money having been advanced to members. It was hoped that next year they would be able to increase the dividend, as money which was bringing in only one per cent last year was now producing considerably more. Mr GREALEY moved the adoption of the report. Mr STARKEY seconded, and the motion was unani- mously carried.

THE DIVIDEND.

On the motion of Mr GRELLEY. recorded by Mr CH/R- -NOCIK, it was resolved that a dividend at the rate of four cant. be paid to the shareholders on their investments.
