THE EXAMINEE.

JUNB 13th. 1902.

MR. iviORLEY'S SPEECH. IT was appropriate enough that Mr. JoUN :INIon.LEY should have been the first Liberal leader to speak at length since the conclusion of peace. 'or all his colleagues he resisted its approach the most strenuously, and prohesied its results the most fearlessly. He had something of vindication and explanation for the past, a reply to the ~slanders of those who have charged him with de- fective patriotism : We oppose & policy, whm we do oppose, because we believe it is injuriou to our common country, and because we are convined that if this policy had not been followed ic would hays been better for onr common country. ut the origin of the war has happily passed into the domain of history. It is better to leave that 'question for awhile, to "let the lava cool," as Mr. Monr.Ev phrased it, and meantime to bless the Peace that has come at length, and to look forward to the future that the old misconceptions, the old miscalculations which produced and prolonged the war, shall not wreck the new epoch which has -begun. The past is behind us, a past of " unco.sa- pensating mischief and irreparable wrong " ; it lies With uS to build a future on a sounder basis. The 'terms of peace are what we choose to make them. There is scope enough within their limits for generosity and wisdom, but the risk of strife and folly is not exluded. All depends on whether we look to force as/ our one specific, take race ascendency for or guiding principle. It will be a stiff fight. The counsellors are already at our elbow who urge that th 3 spoils are to the victors, that our motto must be " our own People first, our own people second, our own " People third." The younger sons who held com- missions in the war will ask for appointments in the administration. The Colonial "loyalists," whose ruling passion is hostility to the Boers, will demand the rewards of their sacrifices. Everything turns on our choice of _tactful and syrnpathetic Officers, and everything is lost if we appoint the type of man who has made Ireland a nursery of discontent. ``.:; Already, too, these same counsellors are urging us that we must be in no hurry 0 fulfil our pledge to grant the Boers self-govern- Izent. We annexed the Transvaal once before. Then, too, we promised self-govern saent. We lost the country because year after year we delayed to fulfil that pledge. The longer we waited the more disaffected the Boers became, and to grant self- government to the disaffected seemed dangerous. That experience may well recur. lam persuaded that one great reasoa and root of the rising of the Transvaal, which ended in Majaba and the Sah,equent proceedings, was due to the failure to carry out promptly—promptly, gentlemen, and effectually—the promises that both Governments, ,both Liberal and Conservative, had made that they should have representative institu- tions. (Hear, bear.) I press thii upr.n you. It is a vital point. If it is not done promptly—and by promptitude I mean roasonably effective prompti- tude—you will, as sure as to-rnortow's suo will rise in the heavens, you will again have the some mess that was produced by the delay in MD and 1881. Mr. Mormey's warning is one to remember.

TURNINO From the war to matters near home, it 'was not difficult for Mr. INIonLtY to sum up the whole present duty of Liberals in a phrase—" Re- sistance to reaction" is the RESISTANCE TO only watchword which meets REACTION, the needs Of the hour. A Government elected by a trick at a moment of passion and ferment has need the mandate which it obtained to conclude the war to unsettle more than one fixed principle °I national policy. It has tampered with constitu- tlanal liberty in the Cartwright case. It has ttlbordinated the needs of education to the *ligencies of a Church in a Bill for the extinction !4 School Boards and the endowment of sectarian teaching. With a daring even more surprising it ilks assailed Free Trade with a Bread Tax. Of all thc results of the war this economic revolution threatens to be the most far-reaching. It is on1:1 Part of a sweeping scheme to impose a whole com- Plicated tariff upon all manner of foodstuffs and articles of necessity. A preferential duty is to (3111pel us to buy our wheat and our timber from Canada, our mutton from New Zealand, our wool our wine from Australia, our sugar from the West Indies. And since non of these Colonies can .15t0duce all that we require, the only result will ftie that prices will rise, and while a meagre revenue i "4's into the Exchequer from the tax on foreign Ports a vast profit must finds its way t° the Colonial producer. It is admirable to :'evelop the unity of the Empire; admirable, 'lO, to encourage Colonial enterprise. But this

! method is merely suicidal. It is only necessary to ! look at the figures. Oar Colonial trade amounts to £12,000,000 a year, our foreign trade to £q305,000,009. For" an imaginary advantage to a small trade," as Mr MORLEY put it, Mr CHAMBER- LAIN is proposing " to dislocate and disarrange an immense trade." We cannot impose a hostile tariff against the rest of the earth without provok- ing retaliation. We cannot raise the price of food without raising wages and diminishing profits. Nor can we increase the price of raw materials like wool without hampering our manufactures and checking our exports.

But the. whole duty of Liberalism, after all,f does not end at home. Mr Montny had to remind us of two kindred problems that EMPIRE AND still lie at our door—the Old cDRONATION. problem in Ireland and the new problem in Cape Colony. You are going to have in two or three weeks great rejoicing. I wonder how many of the Colonial Prime MinistLrs are ELAII3 Rulers ? (A Voice : " All.") Tney Bra all, says somehxly. I think it will be found h mentlemen. Let me put this to you. We Me going to have, I suppose—well, we may have a proposal—to suspand the constitution in Cape Colony. Just picture the scones in the Lions° of Commons, We all ge^. up and we all make a elequent, passi2uate, argumentative speeches in favour ur the rijlat of the Colonies to govern them- E elves. The neat day Mr. REDAIO7.STD makes a motion in favour of giving self-government in oue shape or another to Ireland. We then all pick cut a new set of arguments. (Laughter.) What was on Monday unauswerable,on Tuesday becomes not worth men- tioning, (Laughter.) What was on Monday a sacred principle of self-governmcnt becomes on Tuesday mere moonshine and claptrap. (Laughter.) That is a comedy in which I, itt least, do not pro- pose to take any part. Tne Boers are t 3 have self- government in order to make them loyal ; the Irish are not to have it because they are disloyal. There is only one sound priaciple on which all these problems can be solved. Loyalty can be WO.l only by freedom. The Empire is strong where it rests on the consent of the governed, weak where it it upheld by force. We cannot refuse self- government to the Irish and the Cape Boers and grant it to the Boers of the ;Transvaal. The Liberal principle admits no exception.

Seldom has a grant to a successful soldier been better earned than that which the House of Com- mons has just voted to Lord Kitchener. He had to grapple with a task at once more thankless and more difficult than that of Lord Roberts. He did not tire of it until it was accomplished, and all the while that he made war with restless energy he so conducted it as to make peace possible. Had he not resisted the powerful party at home which would have urged him to "severe measures," even to the refusal of quarter, the war could never have ended ,as it has done. At his first meeting with General Botha, a year ago, he sowed the seeds of a confidence which has since borne fruit. Had not Mr. Chamberlain then ridiculed as " preposterous " suggestions which have since been accepted, it is even possible that peace might have surprised us in 1901. Lord Kitchener has shown the sort of tact and wisdom which Wellington Tr ore than once displayed. A soldier who really meets a brave enurny learns to respect him long before the civilian, who feeds on biassed news at home. The Boers have not been backward in reciprocating his esteem. They trust him, and that sentiment has counted for much in their acceptance of peace. Indeed, things have gone so far that we find General De Wet urging his men to become as good colonists as they once were burghers. It is good news that Lord Kitchener holds that his duty lies in South Africa, when he might well have come home to the Coronation. His presence is a guarantee that the manly and kindly policy will prevail.

Sir Michael Hicks-Beach has disappointed those who imagined that the coming of peace would bring with it any immediate alleviation of our burdens. Both the new taxes are to stand. The Income Tax paver will pay more next year than last, and the Bread Tax remains to aggravate the poverty of the very poor. The Bread Tar, with its miserable yield of two and a-half millions, is valuable not for what it brings into the E tchequer, but for the principle it establishes. It revives protection, and marks a definite break with Free Trade. It does not, indeed, confer a benefit on the English farmer so substantial as he enjoyed under the old Corn Laws. It is a tax solely on imported wheat, which raises the market-price all round, and thus enables the home producer to pocket an artificial profit. That profit may be large or small, but so long as the tax is levied only on the foreign article the principle remains the same. The public pays a tax on every loaf and the Exchequer, the farmers, and the bakers divide the spoils. The charm of this innovation lies in the fact that it will enable Mr. Chamberlain to meet the Colonial Premiers in a few weeks with a basis on which a preferential tariff can be negoti- ated. Next year we shall be invited to exempt Canadian as we exempt British wheat. And if the public will stand a Corn Tax and a Sugar Tax, why not a Meat Tax, from which in due course Australia would profit? All the Colonial Premiers have approved the scheme; and if they claim a preferential tariff as their reward for the assist- ance they have gLven us in this war, it will not be easy to resist them gracefully. In the long run preferential trade with the Colonies means that Europe will retaliate with hostile tariffs against us, and we shall find that we have abandoned two- thirds of our trade in order to secure the remain- ing one-third. The Colonies hale everything to gain from such an arrangement, but to us it could only mean dear food, slack trade, and in the long run depopulation.

When the definite pronouncement was made that the" long pull" had been abolished by War rington licensed victuallers we accepted the state ment without question. It now appears that there has been no such thing as abolition but merely a " regulation " of the "tong pull." This fact transpired at a meeting of the Assessment Committee last week. The magistrates and police, we learn fnr the first time, have agreed to a regulated " long pull" of half a gill. This means that every customer who fetches his liquor to be consumed off the premises receives half a gill over and above the quantity ordered. This regulation applies only to tho outdoor trade, for no overplus is allowed to those who consume drink on licensed eramises. The "long pull" system has little to recommend it and even when regulated leaves the door open for abuses.On the admission of a licensee it seems that competition has led her to give nearly three gills tor a pint, and the same condition of things no doubt prevails in other districts. The system is a reprehensible one and the soonero is completely. abolished, the better for the trade and the public

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY JUNE 13, 1902

===== 0002601_19020613_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

Thanksgiving service reports would no doubt fill the paper to-day, to the exclusion of every other kind ot report, if permitted so to do. The accounts which appear must of course, be taken as repre- sentative of the whole ; from that of the glorious service held in St Paul's Cathedral, and attended by the King and Queen, to those of the smallest con- gregations in the humblest hamlets and villages of the provinces. Never was a national thanksgiving for the restoration of peace more hearty, universal or devout. Apart, too, from the gratefulness evinced for the return of peace, the nation as a whole feels doubly thankful that it should have come on the eve of another event that will be of the greatest possib'e interest both to the Kingdom and the Empire, namely, tne Coronation of King Edward and Queen Alexandra. Now that Britons are on the best of terms with all the world,they will of course, enter with; more thoroughness into the festivities in store for them at the end of the pre- sent month. Since peace was proclaimed moet readers will have been watching the news of Boer surrenders with lively interest. It is gratifying to note how steadily and regularly the burghers have been coming in, giving up their arms,disclosing the hiding places of secret stores of ammunition, and generally fraternising with the redoubtable Tommy Atkins. Like;ourselves, they all seem glad to have done with war, and none but a brave and dogged race could or would have held out so long. It is a little interesting to speculate as to the effect which the prohibition of carrying arms will have upon such a people, the expert use of the rifle being one of the things nearest and dearest to the heart of the Boer. Having forfeited British confidence, for a time at least, possibly the privilege may be restored when:chat confidence has been fairly earned and in the meantime he may become accustomed to the very necessary measure of precaution and find be can bear up against it very well.

Much disappointment has been caused in some of the school board districts with the very limited children's holiday allowed in Coronation week. It appears that in a few cases it will be confined to Thursday and Friday, although in most, it is believ- ed, the whole week will be allowed. The limitation does not compare favourably with the liberal allow- ance made by certain School Boards in this direc- tion the year round. In some parts of the year parents have good reason to complain of the length ofi the ,holidays, and for whose convenience this matter is arranged it is for the school boards them- selves to determine. In Coronation week there will really be something for the young people to see and to talk and marvel about, and it is questionable if even the schools in question are kept open, there will be much very useful work done in the class- rooms. Mental concentration in a time of universal rejoicing and festal preparation is probably more than could be expected from the ordinarily consti- tuted school boy or girl. This is an event of a life- time, and seems to require that there should ba no half-measures about it.

A good deal of interest appears to centre in the kind of flower- to he regarded as "the only genuine" Coronation Flower. It seems a pity that this important point has not been officially settled; at any rate to the time of writing. But inquiry in the meantime among leading florists, reveals the fact that large supplies of red and white roses are now kept back with the intention of their being produced about a week hence; that is to say, within a week of the Coronation. That is what might have been expected, since June is proverbi- ally, and naturally, " the month of roses," and individual taste has necessarily to be somewhat largely taken into consideration. The Rev J LI Pemberton, president of the National RO3B Society, has an article in the current number of" The Coun- try," in which he sets forth, rather plausibly, that the rose is naturally the Coronation flower, that during the month" she will decorate Great Britain and Ireland with masses, festoons and streamers of roses," and that, moreover, 'the rose is popular," All the same, if the man in the street finds the cost of a rose, which Mr Pemberton says is within the reach of all, too dear, he will not hesitate to secure the more economical cornflower, narcissus, and geranium. which, after all, form the three national colours. Toe most ardent admirers of the rose can hope for; under the possible and probable circum- stances, is that the domination of June's chiefeet and choicest bloom may not be very seriously challenged.

This most eventful month should be the busiest of all the year for local photographers, amateur and professional, who do not wish future days to be fraught with regrets as to a rare opportunity missed. Everywhere in the provinces, arrange- ments having been made, and in most places being already well in hand, for right loyally and heartily celebrating the Coronation of the King end Queen on the 2fith and 27th, ti,pects of peculiar and special interest will present themselves to the mind of the duly alert knight of the camera, that should be well worth making the most of, and be carefully preserved. Indeed, so important a feature of the great occasion is this regarded in some parts of the country, that the local authorities have had under coneiderat.cn the propriety of securing photographic mementoes which would undoubtedly become of historical interest, and possibly of great importance. Whatever other similarly constituted authorities may think of the suggestion, amateur photo- graphers should at once prepare themselves to take advantage of the incidents of the Coronation pro- ceedings, with a view to securing records for them- selves, their families, or their friends. But to aspir- ing amateurs there is little time left for prepara- tion, if they care to fill their albums with suitable pictures bearing upon this one event of a lifetime, which shall be handed down to their children,and grand children or shall be presented to some class, society, or association which they value as 1116111- bars, to be preserved and " kept in remembrance " in that way.

The Boys' Brigade to-day will muter to the num- ber of eleven thousand or more at what is called the "Coronation Review" by the Prince of Wales in London. The provinces will bs well and numer- ously represented. There will be two brigades of four battalions each, and the organizations taking part in the review will comprise the Boys' Brigade, Cnurch Laos' Brigade, London Diocesan Church Lads' Brigade, and the Catholic Boys' Brigade.

Reverting to a notice in this letter some weeks ago of steps taken to prevent the misuse of the Royal Standard in the provinces, at the Coronation celebration, it may be well to make note also of the following which has appeared in the "Morning Post :—" The Rev P Husband, vicar of St. Michael's, Folkestone, wrote to Sir Francis Rnollys, Private Secretary to the King, in regard to the pro- hibition against flying the Royal Standard, except on the occasion of the Coronation, The rev gentle- man pointed oat that his poor congregation had purchased a Royal Standard at a cost of £lO think- ing they would be able to fly the flag from the church tower as usual, and asked that an excep- tion to the rule might be made in regard to build- ings connected with the State, such as churches, especially on the occasion of Royal anniversaries. Mr Husband has received the following reply : " Buckingham Palace, 4th June. Dear Sir,—ln reply to your letter, I am afraid that the Royal Standard, which is the King's personal flag,can only be hoisted at the Coronation. If permission were given in one case, it would be impossible to refuse it in any others. I must remind you that you can always fly the Union Jack.—Yours faithfully, F KNOLLYS.

Every bread and butter consumer is interested in the fact that this week the Chancellor of the Ex- chequer has carried in committee of the House of Commons his registration duty on corn, which will now constitute a" permanent" tax upon bread. until the Liberals are returned to power and remove it, as they did before. The butter can wait, one "sop " at a time to the farmers, in however, questionable a form it may come, being as such as ever the present Government can safely venture upon, its great and sometimes unwieldy majority notwithstanding. The familiar plea that the new tax is a very little one has been repeated by Sir Michael Ricks-Reach aril a certain section of the press ad nauseam. Like Mercutio's stab, "'Twill serve." The corn duty as everybody knows, al- though objectionable:because it forces from the very poorest consumers a contribution far in excess of that which will find its way to the national ex- chequer, is also open to objection on principle. It is the re-introduction of the system of taxation of food which was formerly adopted by the country, and had to be abandoned under the pressure of famine and the protests of a starving people. It is a case of putting the clock back with a vengeance. 1 Yet the Opposition in the House of Commons on 1 Tuesday evening were taunted by Minieterialists

===== 0002601_19020613_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
Eetters to the Libitor.

Ihe Free Churches and National Celebrations.

Sir,—Nearly five years have elapsed since I left the good old town of Runeern bue I have not ceased to be interested in its local affair. Toe discussion respecting the forthcoming celehration has awakened my interest. It gives me pleasure to know that at a general meetieg of the town it was decided by an over eehehning majority that the order of the Sunday School procession should be determined by ballot. A more equitable way of settling a matter of some little dr licacy I think does not ezist,and in my opinion all reasonable and fair minded men will be willing to abide by the result. I have read the remarks in the " Parish Msgizine " with considerable surprise and I think them quite misleading. I have a lively recollection of what took place in 1897. The fact is that the Branswick School which I believe is admitted to be the oldest in the town, gave no its right of seniority and the ballot was not pressed by the Nonconformist Churches. A great deal was yielded in me interest of a "godly union and concord." But what followed? The morning procession was formed at the Town Hall. Mr Taylor, the chairman of the Council, was an avowed adherent of my church and I thought it would be appropriate for me to walk abreast of him. But there was an evident determination on the part of the ministers of the Established Church to take a foremost position both on his right and left. ' Some junior curates immediately followed and also a lay missionary who was one of their subordinate workers. This lay missionary was asked by what right ha took precedence of the Free Church Ministers and he had the arrogant ignorasce to say that he took it by the richt conferred on him by the Bishop of Cnester. Both my colleague and myself were relegated to a backward position by force of circum- stances and some little resentment was naturally fit. In the afternoon procession the worthy chairman of the Council courteously insisted that I should walk on one side of him l and I complied with his kied rfqnest. We have not been eager to show" a fighting spirit "but, we have some sense of what is seemly and courteous. In a celebration of a purely nationar character, one de- nomination has no absolute right of precedence. The Free Churches have as great an interest in the Coronation as the church established by law. If the matter be of no importance why do the clergy contend for the fore- most poeition ? On all such occasions I think the Free Churches do well to claim equality and to be satisfied with nothing less. We do not ask for any favour ; we simply ask for fairness. lam Sir,—Yours faithfully. THOMAS BRACKENBURY, (Late Seperintendent Miniat er of the Runcorn Wes- leyan Circuit.) 4Vingfidd-road, Tiowbridge, Wiltshire.

===== 0002601_19020613_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

Readers of the " Examiner" should not fail to read a letter which appears in our columns vis week written by the Rev T Brackenbury, late superintendent minister of the Wesleyan Circuit in Runcorn. It proves conclusively the contnitiou of those who assert that clericalistn is nothing hut a euphonious name for cloaked intolerance.

The Weston Parish Council are to be pitied. At present they are in a state of intense indignation at the action of Major Orred and his henchman Mr C L Evans. The inhabitants, too, are up in arms, and their attitude carries with it the sympathy of numbers of people who have gained a kuovoledge of the facts through the press. Every local inhabi- tant knows the footpath through the fields con- necting Runcorn and Weston. At a certain point the road is in a moo: dangsrous condition owing to the jutting out of large pieces of rock. In wet weather this danger becomes greatly accentuated. In rainy weather desp channels are cut in the road and thus farm t:e most dangerous menace possible to pedestrians. This serious danger the Weston • Parish Council have for months been trying to abolish. But their praise- worthy efforts have been constantly foiled by the action of Mr C L Evans, the agent of Major Orred, the landowner. In spice of the uost pressing rep- resentations the agent absolutely refused to benefit the public weal by repairing the footpath. And, dog-in-the-manger-like, positively with- holds sanction for the Weston Council them- selves to execute the necessary repairs. The Council therefore resolved to ignore toe agent altogether and petition Major Orred direct-offering at the same time to repair and maintain the footway. To the astonishment of the Cmncil no less than to the surprise of all right- minded individuals, Major Orred declared in what has justly been aiscribed as a " curt reply," his intention of supporting his agent's action. There the matter rests until the autocratic Major and his ' worthy satellite are restored to a fairer and a more righteous state of mind, and give the desired sanction. Meanwhile the road continues to be a public nuisance.

The amusing part about the affair—for there is a humorous side to the question is the fact that. " since the whole story has got into the papers " the worthy Mr Evans has roamed about like a rag- ing lion seeking whom he may devour in order to revenge himself upon these responsible for its publicity. Apparently Mr Evans has yet to learn that people will talk and papers will print I seriously advise him to calm himself and allow his choler to evaporate. When he doea this he will be better fitted to express !an opinion than he is at preicmt.

[Br TRIBUNIUSj. If additional confirmation were needed of the growing spread of the intolerant spirit of clerical- lam in Runcorn it is found in the obstructionist tactics adopted by certain individulls who have to deal with the arrangements on Coronation Day for the wahting of the elementary school children. Barely a:week ago:a:semi-private meeting was held by the,heaus of the various day schools to consider the order of procession to be adopted by thern.The busi- ness all tol 1, ought not to have occupied above half an hour,but,as the' spirit of evirsaw in the meeting a fine opportunity for "fighting" it was protracted to fully toree hours length. Once more the obstruc- tion may be placed directly to the credit of the clerical party. The first business was the determin- ing of the order in which the day schools had to follow each other. Naturally enough the masters and mistresses—who only number half-a-dozen— unanimously resolved that the premier place s'iould be taken by the higher grade school as the only re- presentative of secondary education Over the second place arose the contention. At the outset the repre- sentatives of the National Schools urged that they should lead the elementary schools on the ground that the cburch was the oldest and that "honour- able age should always precede youth." This argument, by the way, though stoutly maintained by the church party in the present case as a funda- mental principle, is altogether ignored by them when the priority of Sunday Schools is the subject for discussion, Note, for instance, their attitude respecting, the annual Whit-Xfonday demonstra- tions. An attitude, doubtless, determined by the fact that Brunswick should, by virtue of age lead the van. This proposal, then, was met by an amendment to ballot for position, as being the fairest method for all concerned. It is only reasonable to state that the representatives of the Board schools could justly have claimed precedence on the grounds that they were the ratepayers schools and as such represented the people in a town'E procession ; that they were the largest schools and should lead by reason of their numbers; that they were the youngest schools and should have the premier position because green youth should always be succeeded by the wisdom of age. These just claims, were not, however, urged, the representatives of the ratepayers contenting themselves with urging the claims of the ballot. After a prolonged discus- sion the proposal to ballot was carried,the National School delegates calling, figuratively,for water that they might wash their- hands of the whole matter. • They would not, they said, be bound by the ballot and if the meeting were determined not to meekly accept their claim for precedence they.would, in contempt, presumably, of the proceedings, walk last. The ballot was taken. By a strange fatality tile National Schools topped the list. Did ever fats deal so harshly with one of its spoiled children !

But it was too late. Their fiat had gone forth that they would bring up the rear, and they could not retract their word. So that in the Coronation festivities Runcornians will see, in another d epbrt- meat, the National schoola bringing up—what will soon become to them an accustomed position—the rear.

There will also be provided for the inhabitants a most powerful object lesson, which arises directly out of the foregoing. The general meeting decided that the day schools were to mass in the Market place and there sing a patriotic hymn. At the last minute, it is stated, the National Schools have decided not to join in the triumphant chorus of praise but will wait in Church-street until th ceremony is over, after which they will fall into line with the schools as they work their way out of

the square.Suraly this rumour is untrue.Clericalisen, bad as it may be, can never possibly mean to en- courage the chile.reu to revolt against the town's demonstrations of loyalty to the King.lf there be any , truth in it, influence from some quarter should be brought to bear upon those who are responsible so that the little ones, who know naught about sect- arian bitterness, may not be deprived of j lining in a function so important by the psttiness of narrow minds. One thing is certain. If the Church party persist in such a course their attitude will lice long in the minds of the ratepayers and will sound the death knell of their party in Runcorn.

Oa Tuesday afternoon the death took place at his residence of Councillor William Christie, one of the most prominent membrs of the Widnes Council' Deceased was by profession an auctioneer, and though he had been ailing for some time he attend- ed against his doctor's wishes, a sale held on lion- day afternoon. At a recent meeting of the Runcorn War Relief Fund, it was resolved (1) that a letter be sent by the chairman of the committee (Mr Samuel Taylor) to each local member of regular troops, militiaman,re- servist, volunteer and yeoman, who has done active service in :South Africa during the campaign just closed, expressing the town's high appreciation of the services they have rendered to their country; (2) tnat each man, whether at home, or at present in South Africa, be presented with a copy of the programme of the Coronation celebration proceed ings ; (3) that a guinea be paid to each private and a guinea and a half to each non-commissioned officer ; (4) that the allowance to the wives of the men at present in South Africa be doubled for the Coronation week. There are only about seven or eight wives receiving pay from the fund at present. It will be, interesting to our readers to learn that there are about 40 men to partitipate in the com- mittee's gifts. SERIOUS ACCIDENT TO A tUNCORN MAN AT • WIDNES

. _ On Monday morning Thomas Boden, who resides in Thomas-street, Runcorn, met with a serious acci- dent while following his employment at 3fathie- son's works, Widnes, belonging to the United Alkali Compsny, Limited. It transpired that Bodes, who occupies the position of foreman, was superintend- ing the dismantling of a kind of tower or stage, built largely of huge baulks of timber, and was directing operations from a shed close by. The workmen were about to remove one baulk of timber when a second one became dislocated and fell with a huge crash through the roof of the shed, felling Boden to the ground. The men on the job went to his assistance, and Dr Hutchinson was summoned with all possible haste. • It was found that I3oden had sustained some serious injury which rendered him absolutely helpless, and after being attended by the doctor he was removed to his apartments, at Runcorn, on a stretcher. Here Dr Robinson was called in to see him, and from his examination it appeared that the man had been injured internally. He was, howaver, qtaite conscious, and able to relate how the accident occurred

ALLEGED CRUELTY TO COWS AT NEWTON FAIR. John Riley, of Market.street, Leigh, was charged at Newton Petty essions,on Thursday.with cruelty to cows. Mr Steel prosecuted on behalf of the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, and Mr Leach (Messrs Marsh, Son, and Calvert, Leigh) defended. Mr Steel said that on the 17th of May, Inspectors Print and Lucas were on duty in High-street, Newton, on the occasion of the annual fair,when they saw defendant in charge of ten cows, each of which were overstocked with milk, thus causing great pain, Inspector Print said he had to compel Riley to milk the COM, and one man had to hold the nose of the cow while another milked it. Inqiector Lucas and Mr Dixon, veterinary surgeon, of Warrington,also corroborated. For the defence, Mr Leach contended that tour of the cows were in calf, and it was not customary to milk them prior to it. They calved on the following Monday. De- fendant said the cows walked naturally, and he did not tell the inspectors that they were in calf, and that the distended udder was caused through them being in calf. Several witnesses gave evi- dence, saying that four of the cows were in calf on the day in question. The Chairman said there Seemed to be an immense variety of opinion in the matter. The bench eventually gave Riley the benefit of the doubt, and dismissed the case.

An inquest was held at the Town Hall, Earles- town, on Saturday morning by Mr S Brighouse, on the body of George Brown. 57 years of age, who died at his residence, Swan-road, Newton Common, on Thursday last. He left home on the previous Monday to go to St. Helens, but at night he was brought home unconscious by three men. He was put to bed, and the following naorning he toid his wife he had been knocked down by a cyclist in Blackbrook-road,llaydock. The cyclist's name was Harold Cooke, of Moss Cottages, Burtonwood. Mr Hayward was called in and attended deceased, but his efforts were unavailing.—Mrs Brown, the widow, gave evidence of identification.—Harold Cooke, the cyclist, stated he was coming down the road on the Monday, and he accidentally ran into deceased and knocked him down.—Dr Hayward stated that death was due to peritonitis —A verdict to that effect was returned, no blame being attached to the cyclist. THE THiriD CONTINGENT OF WARRINGTON VOLUNITZERS AT THE FRONT.

As there seems to be great interest in and some

anxiety as to the wnere-abouts and the home-coming- of these Volunteers who went to the front in April last year under the command of Captain B Fair- dough, we may mention that letters have been re- ceived from the Captain and from Private T W Davies so late as :•saturday last, stating that they were a6Dundee on the 17th of May, having left Vryheid for the latter place. They were expecting to remain at Dundee five or cis days, and then pro- ceed on their way home either to Capetownffor Durban. The exact time of depaiture or arrival is not yet known, but it is hoped they will be in time for the Coronation festivities. Thera Is no doubt that the Volunteers will, on their arrival home receive a hearty welcome. These men have had some real hard work, and have witnessed some curious sights, the last being an attack by Zulus on the Boers who had molested them. The Boers were hard pressed and came ince Vryheid with a ff la of truce asking for protection until the Zulus had calmed down. Airing of the latter came into Vryheid severely wounded and bleeding profusely, spears and shields covered with blood,—a horrible sight—and as Private Davies states the more they bled of tlinir wounds the more they laughed and made mirth, this being their way of fighting.

A PENKETH GENTLEMAN'S WILL.

Action in the Chancery Court. An aetion was helrd before Vice-Chancellor Hall, in the Chancery Court at Liverpool on Monday, as to the will of the late Mr Matthew Tas lor, of rinketh. It ap- peared from counsel's statement to the Curt that,in the preparation of the will important clauses were by *esti- nemt omitted, and the geeetion for the decision of the court was whether those clauses could be added to the will, or whether the testator bad died hatostake as to nne-tenthe of his property. Mr Younger, K Coatui Mr Grant Om tructed by Meears Robert DIViIS. Kirconnel, Divies Burgesil and Hatton, of Warrington) argued on behalf of the representatives of the heir-at-law of the testator that it was not possible for the Court to iwert the omitted trusts as it could only be conjecture as to what the test ator intended to ineert. Mr Neville, K C., M.P., and Mr Harold Mather (instructed by Messrs Brewis and Son) of St. Helens), on behalf of the children of the testator's nephews and nieces who claim to be entitlsA under the missing trusts. Contended that there was sutfieient indication in the will as it stood to show that t'stator intended to benefit such ehildree. The Vice-Chancellor, in hie judgment, decided that from certain chillies in the will it was obvious that the will as it stood did not express the intention of the tes- tator, and he fur:her held that the testator'e intention was to have inserted trusts for the children of all his nephews and nieces Eirnilar to trusts actually inserted in the:will in favour of the children of a niece who had died before him. He also held that the will must be read as if the estate had been given by the testator upon similar trusts to tioo2e expressed in favcur of the chil- dren of the deceased niece so far as circumstances will permit.

THE LONG PULL.

Warrington Magistrates and the Alleged Arrangement with Publicans. Before the ordinary busina.s was commenced at the Warrieeton Police Court on M mdav, the Coairman (Mr F Monk') said he saw it reported in the papers on Satur- day that Cafe Img pull waa continued by out-door licensees and that i: bad been done by asrangament with the magiatratee. H. (the Chairman) wished to say that tOa 111. igiarates bad no knowldge w hatever of any , such arrangement. (Hoar, hear.) Anyone who con- tinued the praatice did so at his own risk. The Berieh would be glad if the Chief Constable would get all the information be could with referauca to the matter and Beholds it at the proper time. Toe Chia Camisole asked the magletrates to define the risk. The Chairman said the risk wuli be the refusal of the hesses. This was an illegl practice, and there was no jest bedtime for it. One witness at the meeting of I the Assessment Committee said it was done with a view to increasing her trade. The Chief Constable said he was not aware that it was illegal. The Magistrates' Clerk (Mr G F F White) advised that ' it was not illegal. The Chairman eaid a bserseller was entitled to give a pint of beer when that amount was ordered and no more. The Clerk said it was not illegal to give more. The Chief Constable said that for that r,ason he Gould not take proceedings. It was within the knowledge of the magistrates that considerable negotiations had gone on between tbe police and the people interested in the trade about this locg pu.l. The nault was that an undertaking was given by all branches of the trade that the lout pull as it oncs esisted In Warringten would be abandoned. but that a little over-tune of measure which wag stated and defined would continue to be given. , While that was not in any way endorsed or counten- tanned it was not condemned, and in face of the Clerk's ruliat he could not take proceedings. R.Met ts said that was not the Chairman's point. The point WAS that in a case which came before the Assessment Committee a stat.ment was made by an applicant for a reduction of his assessment that with regard to the out-door trade, of which his business largely consisted, the long pull was still in operation as part of an arrangement made with the Bench. What the Chairmen wish,d to call attention to was that it was not within the knowledge of the Bench that the long pull was in operation even se far se the out-door trade was concerned. ant certainly that it was not in operation by the consent of the Bench. The Chief Constable said that as it wae not illegal be had loser' powerloss to interfere in the matter. The I practice wee not illegal although it was highly immoral, I improper, and undesirable.

Why the Coroner Was Careful. At an inquest at St. Helens on Tuesday the Coroner (Mr S Brighouse) questioned a witness at some length as to the identification of a body found in a canal. He remarked that his past experience made him very careful. On one occasion twelve jurymen and himself calmly and deliberately deal- ded that a certain man was dead; but three months a!terwards the identical man put in an appearance and said he wasn't dead. (Laughter.)

The King's Liverpool Regiment in Camp. On Monday evening about 600 men of the 3rd and 4th Battalions of the King's Liverpool Regiment left Bank Quay Station for Saliabury Plains. where the annual encampment will be held. They were under the command of Major Ford, and paraded at Orford Barracks at Fix o'clock. They afterwards marched to Bank Quay Station escorted by a large crowd. The men will stay at Salisbury Plain for a ,period of six weeks.

CURIOUS EFFECT OF SHOCK -- Liverpool Woman's Peculiar Experience.

Shocke often affect people—usually nervous people —in a peculiar way. The sight of an accident,bad news, the death of a relative, have frequently been known to prey upon the minds of highly-strung individuals, until serious consequences resulted. When such a thing happens in the case of a strong, healthy person,one who never knew what nervottenees is. the case presents a peculiar study for medical men. A rather interesting sass of the kind has attracted attention in the Toxteth district of Liverpool. Up to January, 1898, Mrs Fanny Williams, of 42, Barclay-streat, Toxteth Park, was a robust woman, who hardly ever knew a day's illness. Then her hui,btnd died . a collapse occurred, and it seemed at one time that nothing could be dona to restore her. As she and members of her 1 amity explained to a Liverpool reporter, who made some inquiries into the case, i was only after Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people We7.o tried that her former health was restored.

MRS. F. WILLIAMS. (From a phot by Strickland. Liverpool). Tile reporter met Mrs. Williams in her eon's 1101160 in the same strect. The yeurig man. wit° is a clerk ilia ill-) employ of one of the railway companies, is a keen politician, and takes an active interegt in political affairs in Tontetn district, Btfore Mrs. Williams carne into the parlour the young man gave the tAcwing brief outline o! her peculiar eiperienee _ _

" My mother is fifty @ix years of age," he said, " and up to he time of m; father's death, lu January; 1893, she had been a healthy, active worn tn, Sue had always a robust look ; but after my father's death she seemed ta give way all at once, and we feared there was sovie- tising internally wrong with her. Neither medicine nor change of air had any effect %span her. We began te think we would soon lose her as well as father. " Row did she surfer ? " asked the reporter. " She collapsed all at 0 lee after father's death," re- plied Mr Williams. She got into a weak and de- pressor] state' and her stomach went wrong. Nothing that she took world remain on it, and although we got dyepepsin powders for her they did no good, her food continuing to return. She could not even bear the sight of a glass of beer, which stag had alvvays itr4viously ea- plod before supper. She took so little food that we oten wondered what kept her alive. Then, as a natural sorpequenos, she fell away in the flesh, and was so much pulled down that we 'began to think she was suffering from amber or some deadly ailment. This went on fr,r over two years, until she became a confirmed invalid. She was aleverly treated by a doctor, bat nothing seemed to do her any good, and we could see plainly every day that she wax gradually getting weaker. This is nos to be wondered at, as nourishment would not remain on her stomach.

"Well." he eontinned. " at this tine I WAR pArtilll- - strenk with several wonderful cures which I read about in the newspapers that had been effected by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. One case was so like my mother's that I spoke to a friend in Birkenhead, who had taken the pill, and who said be could give Dr. Williams' pink pills a splendid name as they bad made him a new roan. I went at once and bought a box i The effect Was almost magical, for borere she had floished one boa else told us she felt much better, and she began to look better too. When she had taken about four boxes she began to pull up so rapidly that she was looking like her old self ag,ain, She was able to take her food and enjoy it. without tho distressing indigestion symptoms, and with renewed health her naeurally cheerful disposition soon overcame the objec- tion which had crept upon her. MN. Williams was then brought into the room, and her healthy look and matronly appearance, with the pleasant smile with which she greeted the reporter,fully bore out all that her eon had said regarding her im- proved health. Mrs Williams more fully described some features of her illness.

" When I began to feel bad," she Pais?, t,a nasty trem- bling sensation used to came over me, and my stomach went wrong. I shall never forget what I suffered from my back, or how miserable I was. I did not ears if I never saw food. Many a time I have had to take off my clothes during the day and go to bed, I was so weak. It was a happy day for me when my son fetched me a hex of Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people, and in- sisted that I should give them a trial. PEACE:

The country has hardly settled dom. yet from the pleasant excitement which followed the welcome proclamation of peace, a cessation from hostility after the trials, and tribulations of nearly three years which has scarcely been realised in its fulness. Consequent upon peace have come - *no or two particularly pleasing functions, especially his Majesty's personal and the national essages to Lord Kitchener, Lord Milner and the Army, who have earned the undying gratitude of their fellow subjects by their dogged perseverance and indomitable persistence, without which the British Empire would have stood in. grave danger of being broken up. To Lord Kitchener especially all have cause to be deeply grateful, as he appears to have been as instrumental in procuring. peace with honour as in waging war relentlessly, yet as humanely as circumstancer permitted, and the prompt recognition by his public threefold reward was as mucir appreciated by the nation as by the proud recipient. In the peerage he was advanced to the rank of a viscount, while in his profession his standing of full general was confirmed, and he was voted in addition a pecuniary reward of £50,000. Of this there was generally only one criticism, and that was chagrin that the amOunt was not twice as great and equal to Lord Roberts's remuneration, as it is felt that Lord Kitchener has done quite as much, if not more, than the Commander-in-Chief did. It is also a national regret that the hero of the hour cannot be home in time for the Coronation procession, as no more popular feature could be provided, especially if our newly-acquired friend, Christian de Wet, could have accompanied him. However, August is probably the earliest date possible for Lord Kitchener's departure, when he will return home on six months' leave preparatory to taking up the command in India in s ccession to Sir Power Palmer. In South Africa his mili- ary supremacy will he allocated to General tNeville Lyttelton, who has done consistently well throughout the War, and has won golden opinions. This officer will join with Lord Milner in,

EN. NEVILLE LYTTELTON VISCOUNT KITCHENER B SUCCESSOR IN SOUTH AFRICA.

the pleasant task of spreading peace, and it can be confidently hoped that every success will attend their efforts, as our new fellow-subjects, after their most gallant fight, seem to be accepting the inevitable in a very generous spirit, advised and prompted by their generals. Sunday was a most memorable day throughout the Empire with its uniform Thanksgiving Services, in which the same hymns of praise and form of service were nsed.\ His Majesty drove with her Majesty and the Royal Family to St. Paul's amidst general rejoicings to attend the representative national celebration, which was echoed throughout the whole country. The Cathedral was packed to bursting point, and the joyousness of the occasion was echoed in every feature of the service.

The selection of the Hon. l'vlichael Herbert, C. 8., to succeed the late Lord Pauncefote as British Ambassador to the United States, is a popular one on both sides of the Atlantic. Mr. Herbert is no stranger to the Americans, for he has already been twice at Washington, first as Charge d'Affaires in 1888-9 and again in 1892-3, when he gained—what is of supreme importance —an intimate knowledge of the American people. Possessed of plenty of tact, great charm of manner, and of distinguished social connections, as well as high intellectual attainments, Mr. Herbert's career has been singularly successful, and now, at the comparatively young age of forty-five, he is in one of the most coveted posts of the Diplomatic Service—that of First Secretary of the British Embassy in Paris. The late Lord Pauncefote was an ideal Ambassador to the United States, and it was no easy task to find a successor. The post needs qualificptions not ofter united in one personality, br 3ed in a lar

The King, as most if not all his subjects are aware, is a very enthusiastic motorist. I ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

The Warrington and District League.

The annual general meeting of this league was held at the " Marquis of Granby" inn, Church- Street, on Thursday last week, Mr T P Bradsb..aw presiding. The SECRETARY (sir J Lloyd), presented the annual report from which it appeared that the season had been a successful one. Although they had not been without the usaal trials ,of all junior leagues, notably the trouble with the Earlestown teams, he might say that the committee had sin- cerely tried to give juitice in every case brought before them. The idea of having one good League, as suggested at the last general meeting, worked out admirably, The race for the top position proved very exciting. Burtonwood beating Warrington rrinity in the final by four goals to one, scoring 32 points out of a possible 40 ; WarringOn Trinity being second with 31. Latchford Britannia were third with a total of 28 points. It was thought that with sufficient entries, two strong divisions might be formed for the ensuing season, several fresh applications ha v- ing already been made. In conclusion he desired to thank the members of the League Committee for their kind assistance at all times. He also wished to testify to the work done by the Treasurer, whose services had been most valuable, considering that this was his first season. Too much could not be said of the efforts of the chairman,whose straight- forwardness he was sure they all admired. He hoped to see Mr Bradshaw officiating again next season.

The report was adopted. The TREASURER (Mr W H Smalley) submitted the balance sheet, which shoiVed that there was a balance in hand of 168 6. The balance sheet was adopted, The officers were re-elected as follows:—Chairman, Mr T P Bradshaw ;secretary,Mr:J Lloyd ; treasurer, Mr W H Smalley; auditors, Messrs F Clegg and J Banks.

This concludeci.the business. A smoking colicert was then held, Mr H Isher- wood, president of the Warrington and District Association, being in the chair. During the evening the Chairman presented gold centre medals to the winners of the final (Burtonwood) and silver medals to the runners up (Holy Trinity). Mr Glover, secretary, responded on behalf of the Burton- wood Club, and Mr Houghton, captain for Holy Trinity Club. The health of the officers of the League was proposed in suitable terms by Mr Davey Messrs Bradshaw, Lloyd, and Smalley responding. The vocalists included Messrs T Burke, J Lloyd, Foulkes, Forbes, It Dutton, J Leicester, and Jones, and the items were very creditably submitted. Selections-en the con- certina were contributed by Mr A Inca, and Mr Parry gave selections on the " bones." Mr Pemberton accom- panied.

FATAL GALLANTRY AT WARRING-

TON.

Engineer Drowned.

Whilst engaged in a commendable attempt to rescue a woman who was in imminent peril of being drowned, Charles Houghton, partner in the firm of Messrs Houghton, Burnett and Company, engineers and millwrights,Bridge Foot, lost his life on Thursday last week. A married woman named Alice Bebbington living at 384, Knutsford-road, wife of Charles Bebbin,gton, basket maker, was seen to leave her house, proceed along Knutstord-road, and jump over the wall .opposite the Weir into the Mersey. A woman named Mrs Isherwood was unsuccessful in her efforts to prevent the woman falling into the water, and she quickly raised an alarm A man named Charles Hdughton rushed to the scene. and seeing the perilous position in which Mrs Bobbing- ton was placed jumped into the water to try and effect her rescue. Unfortunately he sank and his dead body was recovered shortly before ten o'clock. The woman's husband also entered the water and made an ineffectual attempt to rescue his vrife. However, a sluice man named Charles Gatley brow;ht the woman to the side by means of a boat hook, and she was lifted out of the water. She was unconscious, hut owing to the prompt action of P.C. F Monks she was brought round by artificial means of respiration. She was conveyed home and Dr Langdale was sent for. An inquest was held the same evening, conducted by Mr S Briehouse. John Robinson Burnett said the deceased was his partner and was 41 years of age, and lived at 546, Knutsford-road, Thomas Brooks, 698 Knutsford-road, said he saw Mrs Bebbington come out of her house about seven o'clock• He saw her jump over the river wall into the water. She had to walk some distance up the road before she got opposite the Weir. He saw the deceased jump into the water after her with the object of saving her life. Witness was in charge of a horse and cart at the time and could not leave the animal owing to the passing of the tram'. Fie saw the husband rush up at the time and also outer the water.

Charles Bebbiogton. bisket maker, said his wife had for some time suffered from mental depression, and for the last two days he had been constantly with her. At seven o'clock on Thursday morning he went downstairs, leavinfr his wife in bed. Shortly afterwards he heard a noise and on going upstairs found;she was miesing. The bedroom window was wide open and witness at once went outside and saw his wife and deceased in the water Witness also jumped into tee water, but the deceased sank, and witness and his wile were pulled out by Charles Gatley. His wife bad not yet recovered from the shock.

Joseph Walker, postman, said he sad two other men got a boat and grappling irons and recovered the body of the deceased at 6 quarter to ten o'clock. The Coroner remarked that the case was a very sad one. It was quite clear that the deceased saw the woman jump into the river, and in trying to save her, lost his life.

A verdict or " Accidental death " was returned and the jury expressed their sympathy with the relatives of the deceased.

The Coroner (addressing a brother of the deceased Mid that the jury and himself had great admiration for what the deceased did. They were sincerely sorry ho had lost his life while trying to save the life of another. Mr Houghton : I thank you very sincerely for your hind expression of sympathy,

The Dredging of the Mersey at Warrington. In the course of his annual report on the naviga- tion of the river Mersey, the Acting Conservator (Vice Admiral Nares) states from information fur- nished by the officers of the Canal Company that the total quantity of silt dredged from the canal during the year 1901 was 1,222,772 tons. In giving him this iaforccation he says the Ship Canal Com- pany state as their opinion that the whole of this quantity of silt and detrius would have eventually bound its way into the estuary of the Mersey had not the canal works been interposed. In the length of the river Mersey between the western boundary of Messrs Monks, Hall, and Company's works at Bank Quay, Warrington. and Howley Weir, 187,920 tons of material were dredged and deposited on land above the influence of the highest tides. No new works of any importance were undertaken in that tidal section of the canal during the year, but the length of the embankment in esmere Port I Bay, abreast of the site eofthe tidal npening, has been strengt:hened by the addition of clay hearting and stone pitching.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY JUNE 13, 190`.

WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE

On Thursday last week the examination in music of the students of this establishment was made by the new inspector Mr, Arthur Somervell. In the evening, there was the usual concert, forming the collective ex- amination, at which a number of friends of the Principal,—the Rev Morley Stevenson—were present by invitation. The works performed at the evening concert were Stainer's "The Daughter of Jairus " and Spohr's "God, Thou art great I " The principal lady vocalists were, of course,senior students of the college. These comprised Miss Hannah Barber, Miss Emma Dann, Miss Alice Mason and Miss Ethel Mayall. Mr W J Thomas and Mr W fl Wallington undertook the parts for the male solo voices. A small orchestra of stringed instruments also assisted in interpreting the scores; the names of the instrumen- talists being:— violins, Messrs E. H. Pearson and E. H. Clare ; viola, Mr G. Johnson ; 'cello, Mr Henry Bond ; double bass, Mr R. Whitfield ; pianoforte,Miss Hackett; I harmonium, Mr W. A. Wood. The conductor, as on I many previous occasions, woe the college musical tutor Mr W. S. Woods. It was understood that the perform ance of the works mentioned proved entirely satisfac- tory to the inspeetor and fully sustained the high reputation which the Warrington Training College has attained in this branch of study. At the close of the concert the Principal in a few nicely chosen words introduced the new examiner to his friends forming the I audience, and made a brief yet tender reference to the death of Sir John Stainer which had given occasion for the appointment of Mr Arthur Somewell as his successor. The latter having responded to the Rev Morley Steven- son's remarks and thanked the company for their kmd greeting, addressed the students at some length on the subject of the teaching of music which is, of course, the purpose for which they are thus trained at the college. Special stress was laid by Mr Somervell on the import- ance he attached to the teaching of children to read 1 music at sight in much the same way and with the same persistence as they are at present taught to read books in the English language. He held that children were never too young to be commenced with in this way, it being capable of demonstration that a child 2ould much more readily be taught to understand and read musical signs than it could those forming the I alphabet and that it was found to be an easy matter to educate children into reading music at sight at the same age as they were taught to read books. Another point on which Mr Somervell expressed himself strongly was the advantage to be derived from care- fully selecting the music or the tunes to be taught to young children. In his opinion the traditional and national airs of the country were infinitely superior for this purpose to anything we had from modern corn- posers. Many of these melodies dated their origin in a remote antiquity and by the evidence of their survival had in them a high degree of merit, the true test of their worth being that they never lost their charm from youth to old age from generation to generation.

Warrington P.S.A. SOciety. The usual weekly meeting of tlsis society was held on Sunday, in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- strest, when there was a large attendance of mem- bers. The Chairman was Mr Taker, who spoke on " Peace." The scripture lesson was read by Mr Warham, and the prayer was said by Mr H Nylon Miss Collins. the soloist, delighted the audience with bet rendering of "Children Asleep." and "The Promise of Life." Mr A Scriven was the speaker, and he gave a very instructive address, his subject being " The straight gate." Mr R Wood also addressed the meeting on" Peace," and a pleasant meeting terminated with the singing of the first verse of the National Anthem.

Death of Miss Gaskell, Wilson Patten-street. We regret to announce the death of this lady, which took place at her residence,Carlton Ville,Wil- son-Patten-atreet,in the early hours of Monday. She was, however, without bodily pain. Miss Gas lea's generous gifts and her kind acts to persons leas favourably pliced were well known, and her decease will be deeply regretted by all who knew her.

The above illustration, which is reproduced from an old print, kindly lent to us by Miss Calvert, of Warrington, represents a memorable event in the history of the quaint village of Winwick, whl.ll occurred on August 20, 1811. Beneath the branch cf the giant tree—the Winwick broad oak—a pub- lic dinner is being held in honour of Captain after- wards Admiral Sir Phipps Hornby, who has just returned to his native village, after distinguishing himself in a decisive engagement with the French fleet in the Adriatic. At the present time, when the whole nation is rejoicing on the restoration of peace in South Africa,a description of the event, will doubtless prove of interest ti our readers. The magnificent tree which appears in our illustration, formerly stood in a field a little distance to the south of Winwick Church. The late Dr. Kendrick, who took a keen interest in local his- tory and objects of antiquity, gathered a number of details relating to the tree, and also a number of prints and pamphlets, which may' be seen in the reference department of the War- rington Library. It appears that the Winwick broad oak was for many years famous in the dis- trict for its beautiful symmetry and for the mar- vellous extent of its wide-spreading branches, which, we are told, upon one occasion atlorded shelter from the noon-day heat to a regiment of infantry one thousand strong. The ex- tent of the spreading branches from north to south was 99 feet, and from east to west 87 feet. The girth of the trunk at the base was 14 feet, and at five feet higher it was 11. l feet. The first branch was given off from the trunk at 71 feet from the ground. The broad oak was blown down and destroyed in a terrible storm' during the night of the 4111 February 1850. It bore none of the customary signs of great age, though it did of premature decay, occasioned by the unchecked trampling of cattle round its base.. It was on August 26th, 1811, that the tree obtained local celebrity by the holding of a public,' dinner beneath its stn:de, in honour of Admiral Sir Phipps Hornby, son of the Rev Geoffrey Hornby, Rector of Winwick, who was then captain of the English ship " Volage." The dinner was given in commemoration of Captain Hornby's heroic con- .duct, and success in capturing the French line-of- battle ship " Corona " off the island of Lissa in ne .Adriatic on the 13th March, 1811. The" Volage" formed one of a squadron of five vessels under Captain Hoste, which completely des- troyed a much superior French fleet, cap- turing four and compelling the remainder to seek safety by an inglorious flight. The num- ber of men in the British squadron was barely SOO with 208 guns, whilst the French numbered 2,:00 men and 45-1 guns. The French commodore, Du. bordien, fell during the battle. The flag of the :French ship" Corona," which surrendered to the Volago," was deposited in Winwick Church. A xicturesque description of the dinner to Captain Phipps Hornby was published in pam- phlet form by' J. Wilson, Macclesfield" Courier". Office, and was subsequently reprinted, with slight alterations by ,J. Haddock, the well-known War- rington printer. Both pamphlets are preserved in the Warrington Reference Library. The first- one reads as,lollows On Monday the 26tb instant, this tree of renown wet honoured at the village of Winwick, in a man- ner as-signal as its services have been b.elliant. It was made the scene of a festal clay, in commemora- tion of the late great and gallant action fought in the Adriatic by the little squadron under CJMMO- dote 14,xite. The name of Captain Phipps Hornby, the son of the highly respectable Rector of the I

parish of Winwick, is already familisr to the public eye in the despatches of his commander; and entitled to the love of tho Britiss empire in remuneration of the bravo part he acted in the bay of LiS3a. His arrival in his native village, after five years of absence, in early youth, in foreign climes, in the service, of his country has become an feta to the natives of the place, which will never b 3 forgotten in the memory of man, as his service i will never be obliterated from the page of history. At midnight, on the 9i.h of August, the balls of the venerable church announced his approach, and a few minutes pre- sented the hero to the embraces ot his family circle. Thu respect of the in!iabitants, even at that silent hour, was upon the alert; the horses were taken from hie carriage, and he 1,748 drawn in triumph to the mansion of his ancestors.

lOn the ensuing day a deputation of the inhabi- tants waited upon the welcome straavr, to off.r their congratulations upon the safety of his per- son, amid to convey the wishes of the parish at large that he would honour with his presence a public festival, to be celebrated upon the 26th of August, under the brad colic of Winwick, in com- memoration of the victory of Liss a ; and as a per- sonal tribute of their respect for himself and his lam ily. _ _

This tree consists of a perpendicular stem of the height of about twelve feet; and from its top spread out a number of horizontal braocees, Lirraing a magnificent. natural canopy. covering an area of ground one hundred yards in circumference, and capable of sheltering one thousand people. -Around this veteran of the forest was constructed a superb awning, with a commodious square iuclosure of corresponding aspect. The interior was covered with fine white cotton cloth, and decorated with evergreens, the emblems of Britain's naval fame, and with Rowers, the indications of the joy which displayed itself upon the occasion. Its top was ennobled by a signal, well known throughout the world, the British tag. Three lengths of dinner tables were formed along three sides of the square, whilst the fourth was left open for the ingress of the company, and the accommodatiou of the populace, who arrived from all parts to be spectators of the happy scene. For the preQervation of good order, a party of the Warrington Local Militia .occupied the grouud in front. In front of the entrance side appeared an ornamental column, surmounted by an Imperial Crown. In the centre of the opposite quarter, des- tined to be the seat of the hero of the day, was exhibited the French eagle, submissive to the British conqueror, the trophy won by gallant Horaby—the colours of the "Corona," which struck to him in the action of Lissa. This memorial will be preserved for the admiration of future genera- tions of Englishmen, honourably deposited in the Parish Church of Virinwick. For the regale of the company expected, a dinner and dessert were pro- vided, consisting of all the luxuries of the season; and wines of the most excellent quality waited to diffuse their happy exhilaration through hearts already warm with gratitude and loyalty. This part of the preliminarie4 did equal honour, with the architecture of the pavilion, to the superintend- ing committee. . - -

The officers were as necessary as the society itself, but the individual members were of greater importance,for the successful carry- ing on of the PS A work. The work and services during the past year had been of a very cheering character. The meetings had been longer and the Epirit of the services had been uplifting. The intro- duction of Sankey's hymn book had made a marked improvement in the singing. The year closed with an enrolled mernbe!ship of 572, and an average attendance of .380. The membership was 10 more. and the average 40 more than in previous years. The committee offered priz-3s to members bringing 12 new members during the yesr. Re was sorry that a prize was not gained, but he was glad to say that several memb aril brought two or three new mem- bers each. Uuring the year 707 priz s had been obtainei, 330 the Summer half-year and 377 for the winter half, making the total number of prizes gained during the 9 years 8,540. The sick club had 65 members and was a great help in sickness. The club gave to its members a return of 33 at the end of the year. The chair, as foreman of the committee, was ably filled upon the occasion by the Rev Giles Chippin- dall, the curate of Winwick, whose attention to the guests denoted him a man formed for the occasion; whilst the address of the Rev Mr Barlow, Master of the Free Grammar School of Winwick, and Mr Thomas Sumner of the same place, presented a grateful counterpart upon the occasion. The elo- quence of the grape, in succession to a most pleas- ing repast, which the excellency and number of the attendants made not inferior to a private meal, ex- pressed itself in a number of appropriate toasts. (here follows a list of toasts which were honoured.

The most excellent and far-famed band of glee singers from Leigh attended ; now enlivening, and now affecting the company with a variety of pieces, replete with loyalty, sentiment and humour. Four gentlemen of the company, in addition, gratified the auditors with as many excellent songs of original composition.

As the different toasts in succession involved the names of individuals present, several of the gentlemen respectively addressed the company to the following effect. A :number of speeches are here re-produced verbatim.

In the course of his response to the toast of his health Captain Phipps Hamby said :—The fulfilment of my duty, which has already won me your dis- tinguished approbation, I shall never, whilst I have breath, desert. With my best, my warmest thanks for the honour you have done me, I can only say it is my intention to join my ship the moment she is tit for service, and seek again the enemies of my country.

Fhe olier speakers included the Earl of Derby, who in the course of a felicitous speech said : Gentlemen,it is no common fame which will always follow the recollection of the action of Lisa. The thing would almost be in itself incredibleg itwere not for the many almost incredible precedents which are known to grace our naval anuals. A far superior force to the enemy was defeated by a small squadron of four British frigates. Most proudly do I congratulate my gallant relative tor the bravery and Moll which he has exhibited upon the ever memorable oleasion. Most heartily do I congratulate him upon his safety, upon his return and presence against us, most highly do I com- mend the resolution, which in such a brave and heartfelt manner he has avowed in your hearing, that of making himself useful to the country that gave him birth. Gentlemen, be has told you he will return to his ship the moment she is tit for service. At present she is completely riddled, and that time is left for the repose of our friend which, though he has told you he courts it not, full well he merits.

THE EXAM IN ER FRIDA Y JUNE 13, 1902

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Church of the Sacred Heart.

Rev Father Kavanagh, C.S.R., of Liverpool, preached the annual sermons in aid of the schools connected with this church on Sunday. In the morning the rev, gentle- man took as his teat " And thou shalt call his name Jesus." In concluding his sermon he said no matter how bad a man might have been, even if he had been the worst in the whole town, there was still hope for him if be would pray and use the resources placed at his disposal by Jesus Christ. tie would lift him up and brighten the evening of his days fly His everlasting love. In making an appeal for the schools the preacher said the responsibility for the education of the children rested upon the shoulders of the people of the parish collectively, and it was their duty as Catholics to see that the children had a Catholie education. The fchool could not be can led on without Support, and it devolved upon them to provide that support. Money given for the purpose of training and educating their children as Catholics was well spent. Die musical part of tne mass consisted of Weber's Mass in G, the solos and choral parts being render° I in an able manner by the choir. At the offertory Kellner's Ecce Panis was sung by Messrs G Newton and Percy George. The evening service consisted of Rosary, Ser- mon and Benediction. Alter the sermon Zingarelli's Ecce Panis was sung in a pleasinz manner oy Miss S Eastens. Owing to the unavoibable absence of MrJ Gibbing, the choir was under the conductorship of Mr Joseph Bite. Mr J Broadbent presided at the organ. There were very large congregations at both services, and the collections realiz•)d a very substantial sum.
