PEACE SUNDAY Thanksgiving Services.

At the invitation of the Mayor (Mr J C Parr) the members of the Corporation, magistrates and officials attended the thanksgiving service for the restoration of peace in South Africa, held in the Parish Church on Sunday morning. The invitation was not as largely responded to as might have been expected in the circumstances, the attendance being much smaller than in November last when the Mayor paid an official visit to the Parish Church, immediately after his election to the posi- tion. Fine weather favoured the civic procession which started from the Town Hall-shortly after ten o'clock, The Volunteers in their scarlet tunics lent a dash of colour to the procession whilst the old-time costumes of the sword and mace bearers added to the picturesqueness. The order of procession was similar to that observed at similar functions. In the forefront was the Volunteer Band playing appropriate music in an accomplished manner. Behind them walked the Volunteers, including some of those who have returned from the war, of whom Captain Appleton is chief. he mounted infantry company looked particularly smart, their Baden-Powell hats with wav- ing cock-tail leathers reminding one of the innovations which tbe South African war has intro- duced in the matter of head-dress. The Warrington corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade was strongly represented, and was in charge ot Captain Waring' chief superintendent, It was noticed that a number of the men had South African war medals on their breasts, which have been awarded them in recognition of their services in tending to the wounded and sick during the campaign wffich has now happily closed. The firemen, with their shining helmets and great coats looked ready for immediate service. Behind them walked a posse of police under the direction of the Chief Constable who marshalled the proces• sion with his usual ability. The Mayor rode in an open brougham, and was accompanied by the Deputy-Mayor and the Deputy Down Clerk. Imme- diately in front of the carriage walked the sword and mace bearers carrying the civic insignia. The following members of the Town Council and officials joined in the procession. Aldermen Hutchinson and Fairclough ; Councillors William Roberts, Barton, Burton, Davies, Hulse, Rogers, Peacock, Smethurst, Duncan, Forshaw, Bennett, Tinnion, Malkin, Baird, A J Jolley, A Jolley, and Sampson ; Messrs John Fairhust (borough treasurer), I' liongain (borough surveyor), George F F White (clerk to the borough magistrates), Dr Guest Gornall (medical officer of health), W T Flood (inspector of nuisances', Elijah Place (markets inspector), Frank Turner (superin- tendent of the fire brigade),Robert Young (cemetery registrar),W S Haddock (engineer),Ch arias kadeley (sec.librarian and curator and sec Technical Instruc- tion Committee), C B Aylward (art master), R V L Mathias (assistant engineertelectricity department) A M Ker (deputy borough sucveyor), W Fletcher (clerk of works for waterworks extensions), Mr RP Fairclough, JP, Mrs Clay, and Messrs Peyton, William Houghton, and B Shearer (relieving and inquiry officer), representing the Board of Guardi- ans. The rear was brought up by a ntunber of townsmen.

The procession was met at the church gates by the clergy and choir. The interior of the eourch bore evi- dence of the extensive alterations which are being made. The organ has been removed from the 9,703 C end, and a smaller one is being used near the choir stalls. It was impassible to use toe gallery 03 the north side owing to the alterations, but not withstanding this drawback, the large congregation was comfortably seated. 'rho con- gregation included officers and men from Orford Barracks of the South Lancashire and Liverpool R,gicnents. impressive service was conducted by the Rector assisted by the Rev A S Cameron. A special prayer of thanks- giving for the conclusion of peace was intoned. Such appropriate hymns 89 " Praise the Lord ! ys heavens adore Him " " 0 Gad our help in ages past" and "Now thank we all our God," were sung, and at the close of the service the National Anthem was also swag. Tee collection was on behalf of the;;local fund for the rdief.,,of widowsjaud orphans of soldiers who have fallen in the war. Tbe procession was afterwards ruf armed and pro- ceeded to the Town Ball. Tne route was lined with spectators and a large number assembled at the Town

THE RECTOR ON THE LESSONS •„OF THE WAR. 'The RECTOR took the text I. Kings, viii. 55 to 58 : And he stood and blessed all tho congtegatioo of Israel with a loud voice, saying, Blessed be the Lord that bath given rest unto His peopl?," &c. Having reviewed the circumetaaces under which the words of the text were written, the Rector also reviewed the events of the past 32 nsonths-2 years—so far as the war was concerned. Tony had, he add, now assembled to thank God from the bottom of their hoarte for peace, The Rector asked the congregation to think over the blessings of the war and the causes for which they had to thank God, for it WAS out of evil that He brought good. We had learnt experi- ence in the art of war. We had shown splendid emer- iti:T. but as it Seemed now it was an awful sacrifice of life to send a man in a red uniform to be a mark for Borne of the best marksmen, perhaps, in the world, with a close formation, which was a target for every bullet. They had learnt experimce now, and would have a Plain dress and a scattered formation if war should break out again. War was a terrible thing, but it had been con, ducted with humanity. See how we had fed the women and children, and in these circumstances he thought they had reason to thank God not only for the courage our men had displayed, but for the hurnaeicv of our generals. Then we had learnt respect for the Boer. They had little at first for him, but they hal seen that he could be brave, and that an undisciplinectarmy could fight agaitast magnificent troops, thus showing great courage, skill, and endurance when it was thought the enemy would have been conquered ;ion; ago. It was one touch of nature that mado the whole world kin, and they thought of the words of Kitchener chat if he had been a Boer he should have been proud of the defence the Boers had made. Lot them also thank God for the aff-mtion which had been shown by the Colonies. If it had not been for the war they would neaer have known the love of Canada, Australia, India, and the different nationalities, They ouzlet to thank God for the war which had brought them together. He thought they might be pardoned for their pride in the extension of the Britith Empire. It was a grand thing, aft3r all, cs as it not ? to be an Englishman, to be a meinuer of the British empire, the greatest empire in the world,not only in numbers, but in every other respect. Tney ought to show themselves worthy of that valor'. Lastly, they ouziet to thank God that there would be a good Government. He was persuaded that there was no Government in the world like their own. He had seen a good deal of Contine4cal nations. lii E iztand there were no unworthy restrictiens. and fras long as a man conducted himself pr merly and harmed not his neighbour he could do pretty much as he liked. It would bia a difficult task to make the Boar look upon the Kaffir as equal, but that was what they had to try to do —to give therm equal right i in time, equal voting to all, whether Boer or K iffir. What a grand future seemed to loom up before their eyes in this British empire. They had to bring the people of Africi, the Rector said, in conclusion, to the knowledge of Christ, to try to Make them religious, and all one in Christ, so that there cOuld be a true union as woll as • I asting peace.

ST. BARN&BiIS' CHURCH.

A large congregation assembled in this Church on Sunday evening when a special thanksgiving service to celebrate the conclus'o i of peace was held. The service which was conducted by the Vicar (Rey J H G aollio- worth) was of an impressive character befitting the occasion. As a processional hymn "0 Gad, our help in ages past" was sung. The form of prayer and thanks- giving for the restoration of peace, issued by the Arch. biahoos was used, the special lessons being I. Kings viii., 55 83, and Colossians Hi., 1-16, and the special psalms

103, 121, and 122. The choir gave an effec- tive rendering of the anthem " He maketh peace" (Simper), an appropriate feature being the introduction of the Doxology and the National Anthem in which the congregation heartily joined. The other hymns sung were "Through all the °bagging scenes of life" "Now thank we all our God" and " 0 Lord, how joyful 'cis to see." The Vicar based his sermon on the words" Oa earth peace," and af'.;er drawing several lessons from the war spoke of the blessings of peso°. fie expressed the hope that the British would now exercise the spirit of forbearance and forgiveness t 'wards toe B oers,and then, nojdoubt, those who had until lately been our enemies would become loyal subjeets of the Eing.—After the Blessing had been pronounced the Te Daum was sung as a solemn act of thanks.giving.—Nr T Evans presided at the organ.

WYCLIFFE CHURCa.

r Preaching at a special thanksgiving service in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday eveniug on ' the text "How beautiful nem the mountains are the Ifeet of Hint that bringetia good tidings. that pubffsbeth Peace" (Isaiah 52, v 7), the Rev John Yonge Bud the glad news of peace had filled, the land with a thrilling excitement. Almost everywhere last Monday it com- pelled the cessation of work. , Schoelf were broken up. Works were stooped. Shops were shut. Amid a sudden marvellously opportune' and welcome burst of radiant summer, the nation spontaneously made holiday —all creation putting on the while its mot " beautiful garments." Probably there was a good deal of rsjoieing that was foolish in form and marred by unseemly egteesees. It did seem es if the English people could not be hilarious without being silly. Still, the nation did not go to that " excess of riot " which followed the relief of Mefekiog The whole throb of the national life had been better far than that which oharacterized its foolishly bragging entrance upon the three months, military picnic to Pee- Eerie. There could be no doubt tnat the national feeling about the South Africanevar had uedergone considerable change, and of late the change had been even pheno- menally rapid. We did not need now te open the dice cession of the rig et or wrong of the war. Time would show where truth and justice might have boon or might not have heel. But unquestionably public sentiment had wontlerfulie. ineditiee, to say the lease The language of the press and of the people and of the Parliament has lost its defiant, irritating tone. We had heard nothing during the last weeks of comeelsory ' unconditional surrender, of extreme penalties, of life- long banishment. The language concerning our late op- ponents bad lost; almost euedenly, its abusiveness, its contemptuousness, almost u though some occult com— mand had somewhere issued forbidding its longer indulgence. We bad noted with interest the discovery of the valour of the enemy, of his prowess, of his being !"a foernan worthy of our steel." Ideas of comciliatio had flown into and filled the air, and had purified and sweetened the atmesphere. Jingo songs had been hushed Thus the nation had been preparing, y and longing for the end of the strife, toe settlement of the war and the establishment of peace. Whoa the secret history of the facts that led up to the war came to be writtea it would probably cast discreeit upon some on both sides who paved the way to it. But when the inner faets which had brought about this peace came to be matte known—m.ocm the prevalent idea was altogether mistaken, it wo 1(1 reflect !mein honour on the mienanicaous and pacific spirit of the King. The existence of the war was the last' and the too intolerable sorrow in the experience of Queen Victoria. The establishment of De AA Was the fir it signal gladness, and the peculiar honour of leer royal son acid successor. He would now have the precious satisfac. , Mon of receiving Coronation over an empire that Was once more a: peace with all the world. God grant its long and perpetual conticuence ! When they beard last Sunday night the good tidings of pews. prehiblv many of them held their judgment somewhat In sus- pense, and also their feelings in civet until they knew the terms. Terms of peace might, in largest and most immediate sense, ever be of gupregnest importance to the vanquished. Yet, after all, they could be scarcely of less importance to the evictors, It might not be too much to say that in hardly any previous British wer had the terms of peace been more important to the repute and the general welfare of this empire than in the present case. If Me temper if a year, or even of six months ago had found expression in the present negotiations of peace, even had It been success- ful, it would not only have lift needless bitterness m the heart of the subhead palate but have also left an additional blot upon our national reputation. It was not , without reason that many of them were filled with con- siderable solicitude about the conditions of peace. All the greater might be our gladness, all the deeper our thankfulness, that our fears had been falsified, and our best hopes even exceeded. Toe peace appeared to be " peace with honour." on both sides. Our late enemies were rejoicing in the cessation of a terrible strife, not only because its rigours and its sufferings were over, but also because,although defeated after nameless sacri- fices, they felt they could lift up their heads without humiliation and could afford to shake lunch and b friends. There could be no doubt, and there was every reason to acknowledge it that one of the main contribu- tors to this successful and honoureble issue had been Lord Kitchener. He had proved himself not only a great soldier out a true statesman —a combination as rare as it was valuable. The Boer commanders and forces had held him in respect and confidence, and what was even more to the point, pet- ioles, he had held them in similar regard. His words o;the Boer :officors,with whom he negotiated the peace, revealed the spirit of the chivalrous soldier and of the diplomatic statesmen. lie Cod them that had he been one of themselves he could not have wishe 1 to have fougtat the battles with greater wisdom or (lour age than had they ! That magnanimous utterance should silence the blatant jingoes at home who had heaped all the language of scurrility upon a brave and patriotic En 'my. whom they did not und. rstand. It Wee being said— probably with some truth—that South sefrica had be- come the grave of some great reputation,. Undoubtedly it had witnessed some serious blunders, and some pain- ful eclipses of fame. But there was one great repata- tionithat had Inot been buried thore.Tle B dtish eudiers, rank and file, had come out of the war with reputations for courage, endurance, chivalry, deve- tion not only undimmed but enhanced ! . The estimate in which British soldiers—be meant the privates—were henceforth held, must be abun— dantly elevated by their conduct not alone in battle, but to the enemy and to the women and children whit whom they came in contact. The morzle ef the army had been fitieeenti could hardly be paralleled, surely not surpassed by any army in the world. Our men had passed through , terrible experiences. The earlier battles were tnurder- ous and horrid iu their experience. Led by commanders , who, however gallant and brave, were often unequal to their task, our et:idlers were thrown iato dangers that were insurmountable and fatal. The country was not likely soon to forget the tidings of those early defeats and the long lists of the wounded and the killed and the ' captured. We could not read now the aggregate malti• ' tude of casualties and losses of this war without a shudder. The report of the War Office, issued last Thursdaseshowed the total casuelties siece the beginning of the war until the end of Dday. It recorded close upon 100.000 cssualtiet, and ta reduction of our military strength by 28,000 men. Of the deaths—nearly 6,000 had been in bettle—over 2,000 of wounds— acid no less than 13,250 of disease. There had been buried in South Africa 21,942 resin and officers. These were some of the details of the cost of this strife on otrr aide alone. The local list of soldiers, volunteers and others belonging to this town and district whose bodies lay beeeath the veldt, brought the sorrow of the war nearer to ourselves. We could not forget the devastated homes in this country,the bereaved 'arts in all clasees of the people. Taere was not one of these hearts and homes that would not rejoice in this peace, but oh, how deep must ever be their sorrow through this war!! Nor nouid we confine our sympathies Co our own side. Great as our sufferings might be, in bereaved familiesamid in burdened treasnriet,their native land bad tren made a vrilderneee,teeir homes had been destroyed, their properties confiscated, their dear ones killed by battle and disease. We who had lived here in unhindered tratiquility, who had only heard the echo is of the conflict, and whose own hearts aid homes had not known the agony of son, brother, hatband, father. stricken down in sudden death—we could have ne con- ception of their long agony and distress. Surely our ' sympathy went out to all the mourners on both sides,to all who bad, by tots war, been any wise allcted and distressed, whether in mind, leery or estate. But now, thank GA there was peace it last ! " flow beautiful are the feet of them that bring good tidines,that publish peace." And how real and deep should be our thank- fulness to Film whose mercy had given U 3 and our late enemies this priceless blessing. The preacher asked his hearers not to blind themselves for an instant to the fact that this peace was the gift of God.l7lffevAtlvey had been asking Hint for it ever constantly since the war began! Multitudes had repeated the petition every Sunday from beginning to end "Give peace in our time 0 God, for there is none other that fighteth for us,but only Thott,o Lerd."Peeple even recited ,that prayer inside the churches who clam- eared for war outside. Mtn might answer their owra prayers much more often, and more readily, than they imagined ! There needed en be not only more faith in prayer, but more sincerity, more reality. A great many of our ' prayers bordered upon neeekerie9. What Use, what sincerity, could be in the petition " Forgive us nor trespasses as WS f twelve them the: trespass against us " if they said that on their knees, and rose up to refuse or withhold forgiveness from one another. And What use or reality could there be in their prayer to God for the blessing of ieternational peace, if they left the footstool of mercy, and wont out into the arena of public, national life with quarrelsome spirit and defiant tone to offend and challenge the world P Certain writers in the current press were doing this very thing to- lay. Toe Pros undoubtedly did much to foment the South African strife. It seemed to be already 10-:giare ro' ln to attempt the kindling of another. If they 'c'esired peace they Most be ever pursuing it. " Seek pacon and pursue it." Pelee came from the fear of God and the unselfish love of man. It was the gift of God. They might doubt the divineness of war although they might believe that God, who could bring geed out of,eviecould over-rule war for

good and right results. But war was so gigantic an evil that we could never be justified ia promoting it, or even I in resorting to it until every .other policy nad been per- sistently tried. Must we not understand that this blessed restoration of tranquility was a divine call to pursue and deve!op the policy and the arts of peace, and Co use and improve its opportunities ? It was to be hoped that we should witness a gradual Wing down of the spirit of militarism that had been so rampant in recent years. It would be a dark day for this empire if that spirit should become the ruling spirit. All history proved that the predominance of that spirit had been the curse of peoples and of the race. It was supremely the anti- Christian spirit, A nation could only retain its claim to be considered Christian, as it persistently exalted the , arts of peace and discountenanced the policies of war. llt had been thrown at the Christian Church again and again during this now happily ended strife that her people and tier minister, had been so ardent in their defence and even advocacy of war. There had been only too great occasion for that reproach. llt had been to Wm a painful surprise that even , Free Churches and Free Church ministers had bsen so pervaded and actuated by this spirit. It was right Co be patriotic ; it was right to be loyal It Wai right to be willing to make sacrifices for the defence of our empire and its deliverance from those who would work its injury. But war must ever be to the Christiao a last resort, a necessity full of pain, in which he could find no gratification, iLI which he could ' never permit himself to exult. The choir taco sang the anthem ." fhw lovely are the messengers" (Meudelsaohn).

BErH9:SDA CONGREGATIONAL CaURCH, RUNCORN.

Preaching at Bethesda Congregational Church, Run- corn, on Sunday evening, from the text, "Thinks be to God for his unspeakable gift" (li. Ger. ix. 15), the Rev J Osborne, M.A. (pastor), said these words might without any irreverence be a..kiu ass fitting expression of our rejoiciug over the declaration of peace in South Africa. f; seemed tb him providential that the news of so gvat an event elioeld have CO:113 on toe Sunday evening as the benediction went forth on the Chrietiati congregations of the laud. The news we received with dignified quietness, and deep eympothy, and wittioet noisy and excited demonstration. The rej Acing seemed to have been as great on one side as on tne other. 'Toe terms (Mode settleinene reflected great credit upon the Government. They were just, generous, and magnani- mous terms. Tney reveaZed a clear and strong desire to reconcile and ueite the two races. They were calculated, lee Caeca hope, not merely to pro- duce working (lemony, but peace to the end of time. Let them hope that the, sword which had been laid down would never be taken up again. A gift so groat and pricelees as peace was of a truth from the giver of every good and perfect gift. The hand of Providaoce had beta in the negotiations, and in that earnest desire for peace which animated both sides. What did a lasting peace closiet in ? In fidelity t j the empire on the part of the Boers. Our race bad shown a wonderful p )wer of multiplying and colonizing. A little one had become a thousaad, and a small one a strong nation. God took up our isles as a very little thing, but now Copy Sat upon the circle of the earth. Britain covered the globe with little Britains. The expansion of England had en the whole meant the impanel= of the Kingdom of God. There were many snameful deductions to ba made, but on the whole was it not so ? A lasting peace would also consist in fidelity to the prinoiple of religion on the part of the British. Our colonist's had taken their religion with them. Apart from this our empire would relapse into a godless and soulless empire, where passions • ef an evil sort would work calamity in the • long run. The English were a governing race, and would turn into a domineering race of the ancient type unless they denied themselves and took up their arose.

SHRVIONS.

IThe annual special service* in aid of the Wargrave Day and Sunday schools and tho 'falcon infant school were held on Sunday at the Wargrave Church. Occasion was taken at the same time to hold thanksgiving services for the suc- ' cessful termination of the War. The preacher at the morn- ing service was the Rev Canon Willink of St Helens, and there was a large congregation. Special hymns were sung by the scholars who had been tutored by Mr it Chandley. headmaster. The rev preacher delivered an inspiriting address choosing for his text Psalms 1.26, 3m' "The Lord hath done great things for us, whereof we are glad." He made special and eloquent refer- ences to the end of the war and the significance of peace. In the afternoon an organ recital was given by the organist, Mr Thomas Martin, with vocal selections by the choir. The following was the programme submitted :—Organ solo, " Sonata No 1" anthem" Sing, oh. Heavens," solo by Mx W Carson ; organ solo " Mareis des Role Mages," solo and duet " Come, ever smiling Liberty," Misses Corbett and Molyneux ; organ solo • The Storm," sacred song Miss Molvneux ; anthem " 0 loather whose almighty power" sacred song " Abide with me" Mr Molyneux ; organ solo "Communion in F." In the evening the Rev T Bishop spoke from the words •• On earth Peace" U.s said that war was at the best a most barbarous method of righting a wrong; no tongue could describe its horrors, the suffering death, and utter disorganization. The relief was great, and huge crowds gathered to show their gladness, anl to express their joy and gratitude to God that peace had been declared, Now that the war was ever, the farms that were desolate would be reoccupied. Prosperity would return to the laud that war had desolated. Peace made for properity, war for clestru3tlon. The rev gentleman then aealt with the work of the military chaplain,aad said he had I always been present in battle, in camp. and in hospital.
